Optimality of Approximate Message Passing Algorithms for Spiked Matrix Models with Rotationally Invariant Noise
Rishabh Dudeja, Songbin Liu, Junjie Ma

Abstract
We study the problem of estimating a rank-one signal matrix from a noisy observed matrix corrupted by additive rotationally invariant noise. We develop a new class of approximate message passing algorithms for this problem and provide a simple and concise characterization of their dynamics in the high-dimensional limit. At each iteration, these algorithms leverage prior knowledge about the noise structure by applying a non-linear matrix denoiser to the eigenvalues of the observed matrix, and utilize prior information regarding the signal structure by applying a non-linear iterate denoiser to the previous iterates generated by the algorithm. We derive the optimal choices for both the matrix and iterate denoisers and demonstrate that the resulting algorithm achieves the lowest possible asymptotic estimation error among a broad class of iterative algorithms under a fixed iteration budget.



1
Introduction

We consider the problem of estimating a symmetric rank-one matrix from a noisy
N\times N
observed matrix
\bm{Y}
generated from the
spiked matrix model

[
35
]
:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}+\bm{W},

(1)

where
\bm{x}_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is the
N
-dimensional unknown signal of interest,
\theta\geq 0
is the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) parameter, and
\bm{W}
is a symmetric noise matrix.
This model and its variants have been used to study a broad range of statistical inference problems, including sparse PCA
[
20
,
36
,
80
]
, community detection
[
21
,
1
]
, and group synchronization
[
74
,
64
,
30
,
17
]
.

Wigner Noise Model

The most well-studied variant of the spiked model is the
Spiked Wigner Model

[
20
,
65
,
27
,
28
,
5
,
54
]
, which assumes that the noise matrix has i.i.d. Gaussian entries. A rich line of work in high-dimensional statistics and random matrix theory has studied the problem from various perspectives.

•

Design and Analysis of Estimators.
A natural estimator for the signal is the PCA estimator or the leading eigenvector of the observed matrix. A line of work
[
4
,
3
,
63
,
32
,
62
,
12
,
39
]
initiated by
Baik et al. [4]
has obtained a sharp asymptotic characterization of the performance of this estimator in the high-dimensional limit and uncovered surprising properties of this estimator. One way to improve the performance of PCA is to exploit prior structural information about the signal (
e.g.
, sparsity). Approximate message passing (AMP) algorithms are a popular class of computationally efficient iterative algorithms designed to exploit such structural information. These algorithms were first discovered in the context of compressed sensing
[
23
,
37
,
9
,
13
]
and have been adapted for the spiked Wigner model
[
68
,
60
,
38
,
54
,
46
,
47
]
. A particularly attractive feature of these algorithms is that their performance in the high-dimensional limit is characterized by a simple deterministic recursion known as
state evolution
, which makes it possible to assess their information-theoretic optimality (or sub-optimality).

•

Information-theoretic Limits.
Under the assumption that the signal is drawn from a known prior distribution, the optimal estimator is the Bayes estimator
\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{\star}|\bm{Y}]
. Using the powerful cavity method from statistical physics,
Lesieur et al. [44]
derived a conjecture for the Bayes risk (or the asymptotic performance of the Bayes estimator) for the Spiked Wigner model. This conjecture has been proved rigorously in increasing generality in a series of influential works
[
20
,
6
,
40
,
43
,
52
,
27
,
5
,
10
]
. This formula for asymptotic Bayes risk provides a fundamental information-theoretic limit on the performance of any estimator for the problem.

•

Computational Limits.
In general, computing the Bayes-optimal estimator is computationally intractable in high dimensions. However, a suitably designed AMP algorithm called Bayes-optimal AMP can attain the Bayes risk for the problem under sufficiently high signal-to-noise ratios. For lower signal-to-noise ratios, the Bayes-optimal AMP algorithm fails to do so, and no computationally efficient algorithm is known to achieve the Bayes risk
[
45
,
40
,
6
,
52
]
. This has led to a popular conjecture that the Bayes-optimal AMP algorithm is the optimal polynomial time algorithm for this problem
[
45
,
52
]
.
Celentano et al. [16]
,
Montanari and Wu [56]
, and
Montanari and Wein [55]
(see also the earlier work of
Schramm and Wein [72]
) have provided evidence for this conjecture by showing that the Bayes-optimal AMP algorithm achieves the lowest possible estimation error among a broad class of iterative algorithms and low-degree polynomial estimators.

Collectively, these works have enriched our understanding of the fundamental trade-offs between statistical optimality and computational efficiency in high-dimensional statistics.

Rotationally Invariant Noise Model

In this paper, we study a natural generalization of the i.i.d. Gaussian noise model known as the rotationally invariant noise model where one posits that the eigenvectors of the noise matrix are given by a uniformly random orthogonal matrix independent of the eigenvalues. The rotationally invariant noise model is intended to model noise matrices with strong statistical dependence whose eigenvectors are generic
[
29
]
. Several works (
see e.g.,

[
22
,
59
,
48
]
) have observed that this can be a good model in some applications.
Benaych-Georges and Nadakuditi [12]
analyzed the performance of PCA (or spectral estimators) for this noise model. A line of work
[
58
,
29
,
53
,
79
]
initiated by
Opper et al. [58]
and
Fan [29]
has developed AMP algorithms for this problem, which can improve the performance of PCA by exploiting signal structure. Unlike AMP algorithms for i.i.d. Gaussian noise, the dynamics of these algorithms are characterized by a significantly more intricate state evolution, suggesting that analyzing the rotationally invariant noise model requires new ideas beyond the i.i.d. Gaussian case. Our current understanding of the fundamental information-theoretic and computational limits for the rotationally invariant noise model is extremely limited. Recent work by
Barbier et al. [7]
studies the spiked matrix model with rotationally invariant noise under the assumption that the noise matrix is drawn from the
trace ensemble
. Under this model, the density of
\bm{W}
is given by:

p(\bm{W})\propto\exp\left(-\frac{N}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}V(\lambda_{i}(\bm{W}))\right),\text{ where $\left(\lambda_{i}(\bm{W})\right)_{i\in[N]}$ denote the eigenvalues of $\bm{W}$,}

and the
potential function

V:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a functional parameter for the noise model. This assumption ensures that the problem has a well-defined likelihood, enabling the study of information-theoretic limits. Moreover, an appropriate choice of
V
can capture a wide range of noise eigenvalue spectrums in different applications.

Barbier et al. [7]
make progress towards understanding the information-theoretic limits of the problem and provide important insights regarding the structure of the optimal computationally efficient algorithms.

•

Information-theoretic Limits.

Barbier et al. [7]
develop a general (although non-rigorous) recipe based on the replica method to derive conjectured formulas for Bayes risk under the assumption that the potential
V
is a polynomial function, providing explicit conjectured formulas for the asymptotic Bayes risk when
V
is a quartic (degree four) or a sestic (degree six) polynomial. As the degree of
V
grows, the derivation and the resulting formulas become increasingly complex and a general conjecture for the Bayes risk is unavailable.

•

Optimal Computationally Efficient Algorithms.
Surprisingly,
Barbier et al. [7]
demonstrate that a natural generalization of the Bayes-optimal AMP algorithm (this is the optimal iterative algorithm for i.i.d. Gaussian noise and is conjectured to be the optimal polynomial-time algorithm) to the rotationally invariant noise model is sub-optimal. The authors develop AMP algorithms that achieve improved performance by applying a non-linear matrix denoiser to the eigenvalues of the observed matrix
\bm{Y}
and characterize their state evolution. This is in sharp contrast to the i.i.d. Gaussian noise model, where such a matrix denoising step is unnecessary. Based on non-rigorous statistical physics techniques
[
57
]
, the authors provide a procedure to derive good matrix denoisers for the problem. The authors propose matrix denoisers with explicit formulas when
V
is a quartic or sestic polynomial (a general formula is not available). However, the state evolution of the resulting AMP algorithm is quite complicated; hence, the resulting algorithm’s optimality (or sub-optimality) properties are not understood.

Our Contributions

We take inspiration from the insights of
Barbier et al. [7]
and study the spiked matrix model with rotationally invariant noise from an algorithmic point of view. Our results are not restricted to the trace ensemble with a polynomial potential
V
. Rather, they apply to all rotationally invariant noise matrices that meet mild regularity conditions. Our main contributions are:

•

We develop a new class of AMP algorithms for this problem and provide a state evolution result that characterizes their dynamics in the high-dimensional limit (Theorem
1
in Section
3.1
). At each iteration, these algorithms exploit the noise structure by applying a non-linear matrix denoiser to the eigenvalues of the observed matrix and the signal structure by applying a non-linear iterate denoiser to the previous iterates. These algorithms can be viewed as natural analogs of orthogonal
[
50
]
or vector AMP algorithms
[
69
]
developed for compressed sensing.

•

A key feature of the AMP algorithms proposed in this work is that their state evolution is significantly simpler than that of existing AMP algorithms for this problem. Consequently, we are able to exploit our result to derive the optimal choices for the matrix and iterate denoisers (Section
3.2
). Interestingly, we find that the matrix denoisers that optimize the performance of the AMP algorithm are closely related to the eigenvalue shrinkage estimators discovered by
Bun et al. [15]
in a separate line of work on denoising high-rank and unstructured signal matrices corrupted with rotationally invariant noise
[
41
,
42
,
15
,
49
,
67
,
73
]
.

•

Building on the techniques developed by
Celentano et al. [16]
and
Montanari and Wu [56]
(in the context of i.i.d. Gaussian noise), we show that the AMP algorithm with the optimal choices for the matrix and iterate denoisers achieves the smallest possible asymptotic estimation error among a broad class of iterative algorithms under a fixed iteration budget (Theorem
2
in Section
3.3
). This suggests that this algorithm might be the natural candidate for the optimal polynomial-time estimator for this problem.

•

Finally, our results also suggest a general and concise conjecture for a set of fixed point equations which characterize the Bayes risk (Section
3.4
) for the problem, which we hope can be proved rigorously in the future.

Related Concurrent Work

Independent work by
Barbier et al. [8]
, which appeared shortly after our arXiv submission, also studies this problem from a different and complementary perspective. Using the replica method, the authors derive conjectures for the asymptotic Bayes risk and mutual information for this problem, generalizing their prior work
[
7
]
. The fixed point equations characterizing the Bayes risk coincide with those derived in our paper, providing further evidence supporting the conjecture. The authors also derive the Thouless-Anderson-Palmer (TAP) equations for this problem. These are high-dimensional non-linear fixed point equations that characterize the Bayes estimator
\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{\star}|\bm{Y}]
. Using the TAP equations,
Barbier et al. [8]
propose a natural algorithm to compute the Bayes estimator. While a state evolution result for this algorithm is not available, based on simulations, the authors conjecture that this algorithm achieves the Bayes-optimal performance whenever it is computationally feasible to do so.

Organization

This paper is organized as follows. We start with some preliminary results in Section
2
. The main results of this paper are presented in Section
3
. Section
4
highlights the key ideas behind our results. Numerical experiments are presented in Section
5
. The complete proofs of our main results and additional numerical results not presented in the main paper are provided in the appendix.

Notation

We conclude the introduction by defining the notations used in this paper.

fnum@@desciitem
Some common sets:

The sets
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
represent the set of positive integers, real numbers, and complex numbers, respectively. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
[N]
is the set
\{1,2,3,\dotsc,N\}
and
\mathbb{O}(N)
denotes the set of
N\times N
orthogonal matrices.

fnum@@desciitem
Linear Algebra:

For vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\|u\|
is the
\ell_{2}
norm of
u
,
\left\langle{u},{v}\right\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}u_{i}v_{i}
denotes the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\diag(u)
represents the
k\times k
diagonal matrix constructed by placing the entries of
u
along the diagonal. For a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
,
\Tr[M],\|M\|_{\op},\|M\|
represent the trace, operator (spectral) norm, and Frobenius norm of
M
respectively. If
M
is symmetric, we denote the sorted eigenvalues of
M
by
\lambda_{1}(M)\geq\dotsb\geq\lambda_{k}(M)
. We use
1_{k}
to denote the vector
(1,1,\dotsc,1)
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
0_{k}
to denote the zero vector
(0,0,\dotsc,0)
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
I_{k}
denotes the
k\times k
identity matrix, and
e_{1},e_{2},\dotsc,e_{k}
to denote the standard basis vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. When the dimension is clear from the context, we will abbreviate
1_{k},0_{k},I_{k}
as
1,0,I
. We use the bold-face font for vectors and matrices whose dimensions diverge as
N
(the dimension of the signal vector) grows to
\infty
. For example, the signal
\bm{x}_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and the noise matrix
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
are bold-faced.

fnum@@desciitem
Probability:

We use
\mathbb{E}[\cdot],\mathrm{Var}[\cdot],\mathrm{Cov}[\cdot]
to denote expectations, variances, and covariances of random variables. The
Gaussian distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with mean vector
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and covariance matrix
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is denoted by
\mathcal{N}\left({\mu},\Sigma\right)
. For a finite set
A
,
\mathsf{Unif}(A)
represents the uniform distribution on
A
. We will use
\mathsf{Unif}(\mathbb{O}(N))
to denote the Haar measure on the orthogonal group
\mathbb{O}(N)
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the probability measure
\delta_{x}
on
\mathbb{R}
denotes the point mass at
x
. We use
\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}
and
\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}
to denote convergence in probability and distribution, respectively. For a sequence of real-valued random variables
(Y_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, we say that
\plim Y_{N}=y
if
Y_{N}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}y
,
\plimsup Y_{N}\leq y
if for any
\epsilon>0
,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(Y_{N}\geq y+\epsilon)=0
, and
\pliminf Y_{N}\geq y
if for any
\epsilon>0
,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(Y_{N}\leq y-\epsilon)=0
.

2
Preliminaries

We begin by introducing our assumptions along with some concepts that play an important role in this paper.

2.1
Convergence and Asymptotic Equivalence of High-Dimensional Vectors

We will rely on the following notions of convergence and equivalence of high-dimensional vectors.

Definition 1
.

Let
(\bm{v}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{v}_{\ell})
be a collection of random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We say that the empirical distribution of the entries of the vectors
(\bm{v}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{v}_{\ell})
converges to random variables
(\mathsf{V}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{V}_{\ell})
as
N\rightarrow\infty
if, for any test function
h:\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
which satisfies:

\displaystyle|h(v)-h(v^{\prime})|\leq L\|v-v^{\prime}\|\cdot(1+\|v\|+\|v^{\prime}\|)\quad\forall\;v,v^{\prime}\;\in\;\mathbb{R}^{\ell},

(2)

for some
L<\infty
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}h({v}_{1}[{i}],\dotsc,{v}_{\ell}[i])\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}[h(\mathsf{V}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{V}_{\ell})]\text{ as }N\rightarrow\infty.

We denote convergence in this sense using the notation:
({\bm{v}}_{1},\dotsc,{\bm{v}}_{\ell})\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{V}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{V}_{\ell})
. We refer the reader to
[
9
,
31
,
29
]
for more information on this type of convergence. We say that two
N
-dimensional random vectors
\bm{u}
and
\bm{v}
are asymptotically equivalent if:

\displaystyle\frac{\|\bm{u}-\bm{v}\|^{2}}{N}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}0\quad\text{ as }\quad N\rightarrow\infty.

We denote equivalence in this sense using the notation:
\bm{u}\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\bm{v}
.

2.2
Signal and Noise Models

Recall that our goal is to recover the signal vector
\bm{x}_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
from the noisy observation
\bm{Y}=(\theta/N)\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\top}+\bm{W}
. We will allow our estimators the flexibility to exploit any side information
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times k}
available regarding the signal. We posit the following assumptions on the signal
\bm{x}_{\star}
, the side information
\bm{a}
, and the noise
\bm{W}
.

Assumption 1

(Signal and Noise Models)
.

The signal vector and side information satisfy
(\bm{x}_{\star};\bm{a})\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
for some limiting random variables
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})
with joint distribution
\pi
which satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}^{2}]=1
and
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{A}\|^{2}]<\infty
. We model the noise matrix
\bm{W}
as a random matrix sampled independently of
(\bm{x}_{\star};\bm{a})
, with eigen-decomposition:

\displaystyle\bm{W}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}\cdot\diag(\lambda_{1}(\bm{W}),\dotsc,\lambda_{N}(\bm{W}))\cdot\bm{U}^{\top},

where the matrix of eigenvectors
\bm{U}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathbb{O}(N))
is a Haar-distributed random orthogonal matrix and the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\bm{W}),\dotsc,\lambda_{N}(\bm{W})
are deterministic. We assume that
\|\bm{W}\|_{\op}
is bounded by a
N
-independent constant
C
and the spectral measure
\mu_{N}
of
\bm{W}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(\bm{W})}

converges weakly to a compactly supported distribution
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
. We require the limiting spectral measure
\mu
to be absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Furthermore, the density of
\mu
, which we denote using the same symbol
\mu:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, is assumed to be Holder continuous in the following sense:

\displaystyle|\mu(\lambda)-\mu(\lambda^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq L\cdot|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|^{\alpha}\quad\forall\;\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\;\in\;\mathbb{R},

(3)

where
L<\infty
and
\alpha>0
are some constants.

2.3
Spectral Measure in the Signal Direction

Let
\nu_{N}
denote the spectral measure of the observed matrix
\bm{Y}
in the direction of the signal:

\displaystyle\nu_{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\langle{\bm{u}_{i}(\bm{Y})},{\bm{x}_{\star}}\right\rangle^{2}\cdot\delta_{\lambda_{i}(\bm{Y})},

(4)

where
\lambda_{1}(\bm{Y}),\dotsc,\lambda_{N}(\bm{Y})
denote the eigenvalues of
\bm{Y}
and
\bm{u}_{1}(\bm{Y}),\dotsc,\bm{u}_{N}(\bm{Y})
denote the corresponding eigenvectors.
The measure
\nu_{N}
and its weak limit
\nu
play a key role in our analysis. Expressing the state evolution of the proposed AMP algorithm in terms of
\nu
leads to a concise formula. The following lemma (proved in Section
A.1.1
in the appendix) provides a formula for the density of
\nu
in terms of the function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\phi(\lambda)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}{(1-\pi\theta{H}_{\mu}(\lambda))^{2}+\pi^{2}\theta^{2}\mu^{2}(\lambda)}\quad\lambda\;\in\;\mathbb{R}.

(5)

In the above display,
{H}_{\mu}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
denotes the Hilbert transform of
\mu
, which is defined as the Cauchy principal value of the following singular integral
1
1

1

Under the Holder continuity requirement (
3
) on
\mu
, the limit in (
6
) exists and the Hilbert transform
{H}_{\mu}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is also Holder continuous in the sense of (
3
); see
[
61
, Section 2.1]
and
[
34
, Theorem 14.11a]
.

:

{H}_{\mu}(z)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{|z-\lambda|\geq\epsilon}\frac{\mu(\lambda)}{z-\lambda}\;\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda\quad\forall\;z\;\in\;\mathbb{R}.

(6)

The measure
\nu
and the function
\phi
will be frequently referenced in this paper.

Lemma 1
.

We have,

1.

The measure
\nu_{N}
converges weakly in probability to a compactly supported probability measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

Let
\nu=\nu_{\parallel}+\nu_{\perp}
denote the Lebesgue decomposition of
\nu
into the absolutely continuous part
\nu_{\parallel}
and the singular part
\nu_{\perp}
. Then,

2.

For Lebesgue-almost every
\lambda
,
\phi(\lambda)\neq 0
and for
\nu_{\perp}
-almost every
\lambda
,
\phi(\lambda)=0
.

3.

The density of the absolutely continuous part of
\nu
is given by
\mu(\cdot)/\phi(\cdot)
where
\mu(\cdot)
denotes the density of
\mu
and the function
\phi(\cdot)
is as defined in (
5
).

Remark 1
.

Consider a typical situation when
\mu
is supported on a single interval
[\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+}]
with a positive density on
(\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+})
. The results of
Benaych-Georges and Nadakuditi [12]
show that when the SNR
\theta
exceeds the critical threshold
\theta_{c}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}1/(\pi{H}_{\mu}(\lambda_{+}))
,
\lambda_{1}(\bm{Y})
, the largest eigenvalue of
\bm{Y}
, separates from the support of
\mu
and converges to a limit
\lambda_{c}\in(\lambda_{+},\infty)
which is the unique root of the equation (in
\lambda
):

\displaystyle{H}_{\mu}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi\theta}\;\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:phi}}}}{\Leftrightarrow}\;\phi(\lambda)=0\quad\text{on the domain }\lambda\in(-\infty,\lambda_{-}]\cup[\lambda_{+},\infty).

(7)

Moreover, the corresponding eigenvector satisfies:

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle{\bm{u}_{1}(\bm{Y})},{\bm{x}_{\star}}\right\rangle^{2}}{N}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}-\frac{1}{\theta^{2}\pi{H}_{\mu}^{\prime}(\lambda_{c})}.

In light of the definition of
\nu_{N}
(
4
), we expect that
\nu
should have a point mass at
\lambda_{c}
with weight
-\frac{1}{\theta^{2}\pi{H}_{\mu}^{\prime}(\lambda_{c})}
. This is consistent with Lemma
1
, which shows that the singular part of
\nu
concentrates on the set
\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:\phi(\lambda)=0\}
(cf. (
7
)), and hence consists of a point mass at
\lambda_{c}
.

2.4
Gaussian Channels

We will also use some basic notions regarding Gaussian channels, introduced in the definition below.

Definition 2

(Gaussian Channel)
.

A Gaussian channel is a collection of real-valued random variables
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
where the signal
\mathsf{X}_{\star}
and the side information
\mathsf{A}
are drawn from the prior
\pi
from Assumption
1
. The observations
\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t}
are given by:
\mathsf{X}_{i}=\alpha_{i}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\mathsf{Z}_{i}\quad\forall\;i\in[t]
, where
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{t}
are real numbers and
(\mathsf{Z}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{Z}_{t})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Sigma\right)
are zero mean and jointly Gaussian random variables, which are independent of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})
.
We introduce some important notions related to Gaussian channels.

fnum@@desciitem
MMSE and MMSE Estimator.

The minimum mean squared error (MMSE) for estimating the signal
\mathsf{X}_{\star}
based on the observations
(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t})
and the side information
\mathsf{A}
, denoted by
\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
, is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}],

(8)

where the minimum is over
L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
, which denotes the set of all measurable functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{t+k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})]<\infty
. The function
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
that minimizes the RHS in (
8
) is called the MMSE estimator for
\mathsf{X}_{\star}
.

fnum@@desciitem
DMMSE and DMMSE Estimator.

The divergence-free minimum mean squared error (DMMSE) for estimating the signal
\mathsf{X}_{\star}
based on
(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
, denoted by
\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
, is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}

(9)

\displaystyle\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1:t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\quad\text{subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}f(\mathsf{X}_{1:t};\mathsf{A})]=0,\;\forall\;i\;\in\;[t],

where
\mathsf{X}_{1:t}
is a shorthand for the collection of random variables
(\mathsf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}_{t})
. The constraints
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})]=0,\;\forall\;i\in[t]
in (
9
) require the estimator to be uncorrelated with the noise and are called
divergence-free
constraints. The function
f
which minimizes the RHS in (
9
) is called the DMMSE estimator for
\mathsf{X}_{\star}
.

fnum@@desciitem
Scalar Gaussian Channels.

A important role is played by
scalar
Gaussian channels, which refers to a collection of real-valued random variables
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})
generated as follows:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad\mathsf{X}|\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathsf{X}_{\star},1-\omega\right),

where
\omega\in[0,1]
is called the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the channel. Notice that a scalar Gaussian channel is a Gaussian channel with a single observation
\mathsf{X}
normalized to satisfy
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}^{2}]=1
. For a scalar Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})
, we define several important functions which will play a key role in this paper.

fnum@@desciiitem
MMSE Function and MMSE Estimator for Scalar Gaussian Channels.

The function
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}:[0,1]\mapsto[0,1]
represents MMSE of a scalar Gaussian channel as a function of the SNR
\omega
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}).

The function
\varphi(\cdot|\omega):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
denotes the MMSE estimator for the scalar Gaussian channel at SNR
\omega
:

\displaystyle\varphi(x;a|\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}=x,\mathsf{A}=a]\quad\forall\;x\in\mathbb{R},\;a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}.

(10)

fnum@@desciiitem
DMMSE Function and DMMSE Estimator for Scalar Gaussian Channels.

The function
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}:[0,1]\mapsto[0,1]
represents DMMSE of a scalar Gaussian channel as a function of the SNR
\omega
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}).

fnum@@desciiitem

The function
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\cdot|\omega):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
denotes the DMMSE estimator for the scalar Gaussian channel at SNR
\omega
.
Ma and Ping [50]
have shown that the DMMSE estimator of a scalar Gaussian channel at SNR
\omega
is given by (see Lemma
3
in the appendix for a self-contained proof):

\displaystyle\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(x;a|\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\begin{cases}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{\omega}}{\sqrt{1-\omega}}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi({\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]}\right)^{-1}\left(\varphi(x;a|\omega)-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi({\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}}|\omega)]}{\sqrt{1-\omega}}x\right),&\omega<1,\\
x,&\omega=1.\end{cases}

(11)

Finally, we will impose the following regularity condition in our analysis.

Assumption 2
.

For any
\omega\in[0,1]
, the MMSE estimator
\varphi(\cdot|\omega):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
for the scalar Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad\mathsf{X}|\mathsf{A},\mathsf{X}_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathsf{X}_{\star},1-\omega\right)
is continuously differentiable and Lipschitz
2
2

2

In the absence of any side information, a sufficient condition for Assumption
2
to hold is that the signal random variable
\mathsf{X}_{\star}
is compactly supported (see
[
54
, Remark 2.3]
and
[
31
, Lemma 3.8]
).
.

3
Main results

We now present the main results obtained in this paper.

3.1
Orthogonal Approximate Message Passing Algorithms

We introduce a class of iterative methods to estimate the signal
\bm{x}_{\star}
using observation
\bm{Y}
generated from the spiked matrix model (
1
), called
Orthogonal Approximate Message Passing
(OAMP) algorithms.

Definition 3

(OAMP algorithms)
.

An OAMP algorithm generates iterates
\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\dotsc
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
according to the update rule:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a})\quad\forall\;t\in\mathbb{N},

(12a)

where
\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is the observed noisy matrix and
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times k}
denotes the side information available for estimating
\bm{x}_{\star}
. The estimate of
\bm{x}_{\star}
at iteration
t
is obtained by applying a post-processing function
\psi_{t}
to the iterates
\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t}
and the side-information
\bm{a}
:

\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}=\psi_{t}\left(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t};\bm{a}\right).

(12b)

In the above equations,
for each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the matrix denoiser
\Psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
acts on the matrix
\bm{Y}
in the following way: if
\bm{Y}=\bm{O}\diag(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{N})\bm{O}^{\top}
is the eigen-decomposition of
\bm{Y}
, then,
\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})=\bm{O}\diag(\Psi_{t}(\lambda_{1}),\dotsc,\Psi_{t}(\lambda_{N}))\bm{O}^{\top}
. Likewise, the iterate denoisers
f_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and the post-processing function function
\psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
act entry-wise on the
N
components of its vector inputs. We also require that the matrix denoisers
(\Psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and iterate denoisers
(f_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy certain constraints, which we will introduce in equations (
14
) and (
15
) below, after defining the notion of state evolution random variables associated with an OAMP algorithm.

fnum@@desciitem
State Evolution Random Variables.

Each OAMP algorithm is associated with a collection of state evolution random variables
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{X}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}};\mathsf{A})
, which describes the joint asymptotic behavior of the signal
\bm{x}_{\star}
, the iterates
(\bm{x}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
, and the side information
\bm{a}
. The distribution of these random variables is given by:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad\mathsf{X}_{t}=\beta_{t}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\mathsf{Z}_{t}\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},

(13a)

where
(\beta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
is defined via the recursion:

\displaystyle\beta_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu}\big[\Psi_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})],

(13b)

and
(\mathsf{Z}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are zero mean jointly Gaussian random variables, independent of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
, whose covariance matrix is given by the recursion:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{s}\mathsf{Z}_{t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}{\mathsf{F}}_{s}]\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}{\mathsf{F}}_{t}]\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}{\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}}\sim\nu}[{\Psi}_{s}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}),{\Psi}_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]

(13c)

\displaystyle+(\mathbb{E}[{\mathsf{F}}_{s}{\mathsf{F}}_{t}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}{\mathsf{F}}_{s}]\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}{\mathsf{F}}_{t}])\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\sim\mu}[{\Psi}_{s}(\mathsf{\Lambda}),{\Psi}_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda})].

In the above display,
\mathsf{F}_{s}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}f_{s}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{s-1};\mathsf{A})
and
\mathsf{F}_{t}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})
.

fnum@@desciitem
Requirements on Matrix Denoisers.

The matrix denoisers
(\Psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
used in the OAMP algorithm should be continuous functions which do not change with
N
, and are required to satisfy the
trace-free constraint
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]=0,\quad\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu.

(14)

fnum@@desciitem
Requirements on Iterate Denoisers and Post-processing Functions.

For each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the iterate denoiser
f_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and the post-processing function
\psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
used in the OAMP algorithm should be continuously differentiable and Lipschitz functions which do not change with
N
. In addition, the iterate denoisers
(f_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are required to satisfy the
divergence-free
constraint:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\partial_{s}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle=0\quad\forall\;s\;\in\;[t-1],\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},

(15)

where
\partial_{s}f_{t}
denotes the partial derivative of
f_{t}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{s},\dotsc,x_{t-1};a)
with respect to
x_{s}
.

Our first main result is the following theorem, which provides a characterization of the dynamics of an OAMP algorithm, in the high-dimensional limit, in terms of the associated state evolution random variables.

Theorem 1

(State evolution of OAMP)
.

Consider a general OAMP algorithm of the form (
12
) that satisfies the requirements stated in Definition
3
, and let
\{\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{X}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{A}\}
be the associated state evolution random variables. Then for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left(\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t};\bm{a}\right)\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A}).

(16)

We present a proof sketch of this result highlighting the key ideas in Section
4.1
, and defer the complete proof to Appendix
B
.

Remark 2

(Designing divergence-free denoisers)
.

One can construct iterate denoisers that satisfy the divergence-free constraint (
15
) by designing them adaptively: once divergence-free iterate denoisers
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t}
have been specified, any candidate denoiser
\widetilde{f}_{t+1}:\mathbb{R}^{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
can be corrected to obtain a divergence-free denoiser:

f_{t+1}(x;a)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\widetilde{f}_{t+1}(x;a)-\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\partial_{i}\widetilde{f}_{t+1}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})]\cdot x_{i}\quad\forall\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{t},a\in\mathbb{R}^{k},

which satisfies the divergence-free constraint (
15
) by construction. Here,
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
are the state evolution random variables associated with the first
t
iterations of the algorithm.

Remark 3

(Connections to AMP Algorithms for Compressed Sensing)
.

The OAMP algorithm introduced in Definition
3
can be viewed as a natural analog of the orthogonal AMP (OAMP)
[
50
]
or vector AMP (VAMP) algorithm
[
69
]
developed for compressed sensing (or regularized linear regression). The key feature of these algorithms, which is shared by the algorithm in Definition 1, is the use of trace-free matrix denoisers (cf. (
14
)) and divergence-free iterate denoisers (cf. (
15
)). These features significantly simplify the algorithm’s state evolution. However, unlike the OAMP algorithm introduced in this work, the compressed sensing algorithms compute a matrix-vector multiplication involving a rotationally invariant matrix at each iteration. In contrast, only the observed matrix
\bm{Y}
is available in the spiked matrix model, not the rotationally invariant noise matrix
\bm{W}
. Although
\bm{Y}
is a rank-1 perturbation of
\bm{W}
,
\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})
, the matrix used by the OAMP algorithm at iteration
t
, cannot be expressed as a simple perturbation of
\bm{W}
for a general matrix denoiser
\Psi_{t}
. This complicates the analysis of these algorithms. We refer the reader to
Fan [29, Remark 3.3]
for related discussions.

3.2
The Optimal OAMP Algorithm

Theorem
1
provides a characterization of the asymptotic mean squared error (MSE) of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}
computed by a general OAMP algorithm (
12
) in terms of the associated state evolution random variables:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\bm{x}_{\star}-\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}|\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\psi_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})|^{2}.

The limiting value of the MSE depends implicitly on the functions
(\Psi_{t},f_{t},\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
used in the OAMP algorithm since these functions determine the joint distribution of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{X}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}};\mathsf{A})
. As our second contribution, we use the above characterization to derive the optimal choice for these functions which minimizes the MSE. We call the resulting algorithm the
optimal OAMP algorithm
, and introduce it below.

The Optimal OAMP Algorithm

We first introduce the optimal OAMP algorithm in some simple corner cases, and then consider the typical case.

fnum@@desciitem
Corner Cases.

If
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)=\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]=0
, then it is possible to reconstruct the signal perfectly from the side information alone. Hence, the optimal OAMP algorithm outputs the estimator:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\bm{a}|0)\quad\forall\;t\in\mathbb{N},

(17a)

where
\varphi(\cdot|0)
is the MMSE estimator for the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
(which operates at SNR
\omega=0
). In this case, the optimal OAMP algorithm achieves zero asymptotic mean squared error:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\bm{x}_{\star}-\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{Thm. \ref{thm:SE}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}|\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\varphi(\mathsf{A}|0)|^{2}\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:mmse-scalar}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}|\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]=0\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

In the other extreme, if
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)=\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]=1
(recall
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}^{2}]=1
from Assumption
1
), the optimal OAMP algorithm returns the trivial estimator:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{0}\quad\forall\;t\in\mathbb{N}.

(17b)

In this case, the asymptotic MSE of the optimal OAMP algorithm is:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\bm{x}_{\star}-\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}\overset{\text{\tiny{}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\mathsf{X}_{\star}^{2}=1\quad\text{ by Assumption~\ref{assump:signal-noise}}.

fnum@@desciitem
Typical Case.

In the typical situation when
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)=\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]\in(0,1)
, the optimal OAMP algorithm generates a sequence of iterates
\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\dotsc
using the update rule:

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\omega}_{t}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{{\rho}_{t}}\right)\cdot\Psi_{\star}(\bm{Y};{\rho}_{t})\cdot\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}({\bm{x}}_{t-1};\bm{a}|{\omega}_{t-1}).

(17c)

The estimator returned by the optimal OAMP algorithm at iteration
t
is:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\varphi(\bm{x}_{t};\bm{a}|\omega_{t}).

(17d)

In the above equations,

•

The matrix denoiser
\Psi_{\star}
used by the optimal OAMP algorithm is given by:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\star}(\lambda;\rho)

\displaystyle=1-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+\rho}\right]\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\phi(\lambda)+\rho}\quad\forall\;\lambda\;\in\;\mathbb{R},\;\rho\;\in\;(0,\infty),

(17e)

where
\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu
and the function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
was introduced in (
5
).

•

{\omega}_{t}
and
{\rho}_{t}
are computed using the recursion:

\displaystyle{\rho}_{t}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}({\omega}_{t-1})}-1,\quad{\omega}_{t}=1-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}_{t}}\right]\right)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}_{t}}\right]

(17f)

initialized with
\omega_{0}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}0
.

•

\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)
denotes the DMMSE for a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\omega
, and
\varphi(\cdot|\omega),\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\cdot|\omega)
denote the MMSE and DMMSE estimators (Definition
2
).

Remark 4
.

The additional scaling factor
1/\sqrt{\omega_{t}}\cdot(1+1/\rho_{t})
in (
17c
) is introduced to normalize the iterates so that
\|\bm{x}_{t}\|^{2}/N\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}1
.

The following result characterizes the asymptotic MSE of the optimal OAMP algorithm in this case. Its proof can be found in Appendix
C
.

Proposition 1
.

Assume that
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)=\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]\in(0,1)
. Let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{X}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}};\mathsf{A})
denote the state evolution random variables associated with the optimal OAMP algorithm (
17
). Then,

1.

For each
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\omega_{t}\in[0,1)
and
\rho_{t}\in(0,\infty)
. Moreover, the state evolution random variables
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
form a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\omega_{t}
and, the asymptotic MSE of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}
returned by the optimal OAMP algorithm in (
17
) is given by:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{t}).

2.

The sequences
(\omega_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\rho_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
from (
17f
) are non-decreasing and converge to limit points
\omega_{\ast}\in[0,1)
and
\rho_{\ast}\in(0,\infty)
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. The limit points
(\omega_{\ast},\rho_{\ast})
solve the following fixed point equation in
(\omega,\rho)
:

\displaystyle\omega=1-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}}\right]\right)^{-1}\cdot{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]},\quad\rho=\frac{1}{\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}-1,\quad\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu.

(18)

Consequently as
t\rightarrow\infty
, the asymptotic MSE of
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}
converges to:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\ast}).

Remark 5
.

In the absence of side information, if the signal is drawn from a zero-mean prior,
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)=1
, and the optimal OAMP algorithm in (
17
) returns the trivial estimator
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}=\bm{0}
. Our optimality result (introduced as Theorem
2
in the following section) shows that no iterative algorithm that runs for a constant (
N
-independent) number of iterations can achieve a better performance. An interesting direction for future work is to analyze OAMP algorithms with spectral initialization
[
76
,
53
,
79
]
or randomly initialized iterative algorithms that run for
T\gtrsim\ln(N)
iterations
[
70
,
46
,
47
]
in this situation.

3.3
An Optimality Result

Next, to discuss the optimality properties of the OAMP algorithm introduced in (
17
), we introduce the following broad class of iterative algorithms.

Definition 4

(Iterative Algorithms)
.

An iterative algorithm is any procedure which generates iterates
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\dotsc
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
according to an update rule of the form:

\displaystyle{\bm{r}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}({\bm{r}}_{1},\dotsc,{\bm{r}}_{t-1};\bm{a})+g_{t}({\bm{r}}_{1},\dotsc,{\bm{r}}_{t-1};\bm{a})\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

(19a)

At iteration
t
, an estimate
\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}
of
\bm{x}_{\star}
is obtained by applying a post-processing function
\psi_{t}
to the iterates
\bm{r}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{r}_{t}
and the side-information
\bm{a}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}=\psi_{t}\left(\bm{r}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{r}_{t};\bm{a}\right).

(19b)

For each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the matrix denoiser
\Psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is required to be continuous and the functions
f_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,
g_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, and
\psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
are required to be continuously differentiable and Lipschitz. Furthermore,
(\Psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(f_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(g_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
, and
(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
do not change with the dimension
N
.

The definition above extends the notion of general first-order methods (GFOMs) introduced by
Celentano et al. [16]
(for the i.i.d. Gaussian noise model) to include a matrix denoising step at each iteration. The class of iterative algorithms defined above includes many commonly used estimators, such as those computed using gradient descent or power method, and their proximal and projected generalizations. The following theorem (proved in Section
4.3
) shows that the optimal OAMP algorithm achieves the minimum possible estimation error among all algorithms in this class under a given iteration budget.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}
be the estimator returned by any iterative algorithm of the form (
19
) after
t\in\mathbb{N}
iterations. Let
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}
be the estimator returned by the optimal OAMP algorithm in (
17
) after
t
iterations. Then,

\displaystyle\pliminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\geq\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}.

3.4
Information-Theoretic v.s. Computational Limits

A natural question is whether the optimal OAMP algorithm introduced in (
17
) achieves the smallest asymptotic estimation error among
all estimators
, not just estimators computable using efficient iterative algorithms. To address this question, we begin by recalling the conjecture of
Barbier et al. [7]
regarding the fundamental information-theoretic limits of this problem.

Replica conjecture for the Bayes risk

Under the assumption that the entries of
(\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})
are drawn i.i.d. from a prior
\pi
, the information-theoretically optimal estimator is the Bayes estimator
\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{\star}|\bm{Y},\bm{a}]
.
Barbier et al. [7]
provide a conjecture for the asymptotic MSE of this estimator (also called the Bayes risk) assuming the noise matrix drawn from the
trace ensemble

[
7
,
61
]
with density:

\displaystyle p(\bm{W})

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(-\frac{N}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}V(\lambda_{i}(\bm{W}))\right)\text{ where $\lambda_{1:N}(\bm{W})$ denote the eigenvalues of $\bm{W}$,}

(20)

and the
potential function

V:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a functional parameter for the noise model. The authors develop a recipe to derive conjectured formulas for the asymptotic Bayes risk based on the non-rigorous replica method for polynomial potentials
V
, providing explicit formulas for quartic and sestic polynomials. As the degree of
V
increases, the complexity of the derivation and resulting formulas also increases, with no general formula available. We state the replica conjecture for the Bayes risk when the potential
V:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a quartic polynomial:

\displaystyle V(\lambda)=\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}}{2}+\frac{\kappa\lambda^{4}}{4}\quad\forall\;\lambda\;\in\;\mathbb{R}\ \text{ where }\ \kappa=\kappa(\gamma)=\frac{8-9\gamma+\sqrt{64-144\gamma+108\gamma^{2}-27\gamma^{3}}}{27},

(21)

and
\gamma\in[0,1]
is a parameter for the noise model. The choice of
\kappa
in (
21
) ensures that the limiting spectral measure (
\mu
) of
\bm{W}
drawn from the trace ensemble has unit variance
[
7
]
.

Conjecture 1

(Replica Conjecture for Bayes risk
[
7
]
)
.

Suppose that the entries of
(\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})
are drawn i.i.d. from a prior
\pi
with
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)\in(0,1)
and the noise matrix
\bm{W}
is drawn from the trace ensemble (
20
) with the quartic potential function
V
from (
21
). Then,

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{\star}|\bm{Y},\bm{a}]-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{IT}}),

for some
\omega_{\mathrm{IT}}\in(0,1)
and
\rho_{\mathrm{IT}}\in(0,\infty)
which solve the following system of fixed point equations
3
3

3

When the fixed point equations in (
22
) have multiple solutions, the correct fixed point is the one that minimizes a certain free energy function calculated in
[
7
]
.
(in
\omega,\rho
):

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=1-\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega),\quad\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{H}]=1-m,\quad\chi=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{QH}],

(22a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}\equiv\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}

\displaystyle=\kappa\theta^{2}\left(\frac{m}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}]+\frac{\chi}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{QH}]\right)+\gamma\theta^{2}m

(22b)

where
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
are two intermediate variables,
\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu
, and the random variables
\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{\Lambda},m,\chi)
,
\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{\Lambda},\rho)
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\kappa\theta^{2}m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\theta^{2}\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}-\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{1-m}\mathbb{E}\left[m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}+\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}\right]+\frac{m}{1-m},

(22c)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left(\rho+\theta^{2}a^{2}(\gamma+2a^{2}\kappa)^{2}+1-\theta\left(\gamma\mathsf{\Lambda}-\theta\kappa\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\mathsf{\Lambda}^{3}\right)\right)^{-1}.

(22d)

In the above equations,
(\gamma,\kappa)
are the parameters for the quartic potential function (
21
),
\theta
is the SNR for the spiked matrix model (
1
), and
a^{2}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\big(\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+12\kappa}-\gamma\big)/(6\kappa)
.

On the other hand, from Proposition
1
that the asymptotic MSE of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}
returned by the optimal OAMP algorithm in (
17
) satisfies:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\ast}),

where
(\omega_{\ast},\rho_{\ast})
denote the solution of the state evolution fixed point equations (in
\omega\in(0,1),\rho\in(0,\infty)
):

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle={F}_{1}(\rho),\;\rho={F}_{2}(\omega)\;\text{ with }\;{F}_{1}(\rho)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}1-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}}\left[\frac{1}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}}\left[\frac{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}}\right]},\;{F}_{2}(\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{1}{\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}({\omega})}-1,

(23)

found by the recursion
\rho_{t}={F}_{2}(\omega_{t-1}),\;\omega_{t}={F}_{1}(\rho_{t})
. The following proposition (proved in Appendix
E
) shows that for the quartic potential, the
replica fixed point equations
(
22
) and the
state evolution fixed point equations
(
23
) are equivalent.

Proposition 2
.

Assume that the prior
\pi
satisfies
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)\in(0,1)
. Any solution
(\omega,\rho)
to the state evolution fixed point equations (
23
) with
\omega\in(0,1),\rho\in(0,\infty)
is also a solution to the replica fixed point equations (
22
), and vice-versa.

We remark that an analogous result holds for the sestic (degree
6
) potential. Since the replica equations for the sestic ensemble in
Barbier et al. [7]
are even more involved, we do not provide the details here. We anticipate that the fixed point equations in (
23
) are a unified and concise reformulation of the replica fixed point equations and characterize the asymptotic Bayes risk for general potential functions
V
. This reformulation of the replica conjecture for the asymptotic Bayes risk may be more amenable to rigorous proof than the significantly more complicated replica formulas derived using the approach of
Barbier et al. [7]
.

Information-Theoretic Optimality and Sub-Optimality of OAMP

We can simplify the fixed point equations in (
23
) (which are equivalent to the replica fixed point equations (
22
)) by eliminating
\rho
to obtain a single equation
\omega={F}_{1}({F}_{2}(\omega))
. In some cases, this equation has a unique solution, implying the optimal OAMP algorithm matches the conjectured asymptotic MSE of the Bayes estimator and is expected to be information-theoretically optimal (see Figure
1
(a)). In other cases, the equation may have multiple solutions, as illustrated in Figure
1
(b), where it has two stable fixed points. Here, the optimal OAMP algorithm converges to the inferior fixed point
\omega_{\ast}
, while the Bayes risk corresponds to the superior fixed point
\omega_{\mathrm{IT}}
. Since
\omega_{\ast}<\omega_{\mathrm{IT}}
, the optimal OAMP or any iterative algorithm (in the form of (
19a
)) with a constant (
N
-independent) number of iterations fails to achieve the Bayes-optimal MSE. Such scenarios also occur in i.i.d. Gaussian noise models
[
16
,
56
,
55
]
, and it is conjectured that no polynomial time algorithm can achieve the information-theoretically optimal MSE in these cases.

(a)
Three-points prior with
\epsilon_{1}=\frac{1}{3}
and
\epsilon_{2}=\frac{1}{5}
.

(b)
Three-points prior with
\epsilon_{1}=\frac{1}{6}
and
\epsilon_{2}=\frac{1}{10}
.

Figure 1:
Plot of the fixed point equation
\omega={F}_{1}({F}_{2}(\omega))
for SNR
\theta=0.46
, quartic noise model (
21
) with
\gamma=0
, and
a three-point signal prior
\mathsf{X}_{\star}\sim\frac{\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{2}\delta_{\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}}}+\frac{\epsilon_{2}^{2}}{2}\delta_{\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}}+(1-\frac{\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\epsilon_{2}^{2}}{2})\delta_{0}
.

4
Proof Ideas

We now present some important ideas used to obtain our main results.

4.1
Heuristic Derivation of State Evolution (Theorem
1
)

We begin by an intuitive derivation of the state evolution result (Theorem
1
) for OAMP algorithms, highlighting the key ideas involved in the proof. A formal proof of Theorem
1
is presented in Appendix
B
. Consider a general OAMP algorithm (Definition
3
):

\bm{x}_{t}={\Psi}_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot{f}_{t}(\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a})\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},

(24)

where
({\Psi}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are trace-free (cf. (
14
)) and
(f_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are divergence-free (cf. (
15
)). For ease of exposition, we present the key ideas assuming that the matrix denoisers
({\Psi}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are polynomials
(the general case follows by a polynomial approximation argument). Let us decompose
{f}_{t}(\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a})
into a component along the direction of
\bm{x}_{\star}
and a component perpendicular to it:

\displaystyle{f}_{t}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a})\overset{\text{\tiny{}}}{=}\alpha_{t}\bm{x}_{\star}+{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}\;\text{ where }\;\alpha_{t}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\left\langle{\bm{x}_{\star}},{{f}_{t}(\bm{x}_{<t};\bm{a})}\right\rangle}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}},\;\;{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}{f}_{t}(\bm{x}_{<t};\bm{a})-\alpha_{t}\bm{x}_{\star}.

Using this decomposition, we can write the new iterate
\bm{x}_{t}
in (
24
) as

\bm{x}_{t}=\alpha_{t}\cdot\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\bm{x}_{\star}+{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{Y}){\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}.

(25)

As pointed out in Remark
3
, the main difficulty in analyzing the algorithm above is that the matrix
\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})
is not rotationally invariant. To address this, we express the update equation (
25
) in terms of the rotationally invariant noise matrix
\bm{W}
by expanding the polynomial
{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{Y})
in terms of
\bm{W}
using the fact that
\bm{Y}=(\theta/N)\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}+\bm{W}
. Although this expansion can initially seem complicated, the key insight that makes it tractable is to show that the following approximations hold:

{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{Y})\bm{x}_{\star}\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\bm{x}_{\star},\quad{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{Y}){\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}){\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp},

(26)

where
\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is polynomial obtained by appropriately transforming
\Psi_{t}
; we refer the reader to Appendix
B
for additional details regarding these approximations. The transformed matrix denoiser
\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}
is determined by
{\Psi}_{t}
via a complicated recursion and is not necessarily trace-free. Substituting (
26
) into (
25
), we get

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle\;\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\;

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}\cdot\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\bm{x}_{\star}+{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}){\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}

(27a)

\displaystyle\overset{}{=}

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}+\alpha_{t}\cdot\bigg(\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})-\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}\cdot\bm{I}_{N}\bigg)\bm{x}_{\star}+{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}){\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\alpha_{t}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}}_{\text{Signal Component}}\;+\;\underbrace{\bm{z}_{t}}_{\text{Eff. Noise}},

where the effective noise
\bm{z}_{t}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\alpha_{t}\cdot\widehat{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\bm{x}_{\star}+{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}){\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}\quad\text{where}\quad\widehat{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})-\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}\cdot\bm{I}_{N}.

(27b)

We analyze the signal and noise components separately.

Signal Component

Notice that the signal component in (
27a
) involves the factor
\Tr[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]/N
. Computing the limiting value of this factor is challenging because the transformed matrix denoiser
\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}
does not have a convenient formula and is determined by
\Psi_{t}
via a complicated recursion (see Appendix
B
for further details). The key idea which yields a concise formula for this factor is to observe:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{\approx}\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\bm{x}_{\star}}{N}\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{\approx}\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{Y})\bm{x}_{\star}}{N}\overset{\text{\tiny{(c)}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Psi_{t}(\lambda)\;\nu_{N}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda)\overset{\text{\tiny{(d)}}}{\approx}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu}[\Psi_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})],

(28)

where the approximation in (a) follows from standard concentration results for quadratic forms of rotationally invariant matrices (see Fact
1
in Appendix
A.1.2
), (b) follows from the defining property of
\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})
in (
26
), (c) follows by recalling the definition of the spectral measure in the signal direction (
\nu_{N}
) from (
4
), and (d) follows from the weak convergence of the compactly supported measure
\nu_{N}
to
\nu
(Lemma
1
, item (1)).

Noise Component

The update rule (
27b
) for the effective noise
\bm{z}_{t}
is written in a form for which existing state evolution results apply
[
50
,
69
,
75
,
29
,
25
,
77
,
26
]
. Indeed,
\widehat{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})
and
{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})
are trace-free functions of the rotationally-invariant matrix
\bm{W}
; and
{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}
is a divergence-free function of
\{\bm{x}_{i}\}_{i<t}
. By appealing to existing results on the dynamics of AMP algorithms driven by rotationally invariant matrices, we show that the effective noise component
\bm{z}_{t}
converges to a centered Gaussian random variable
\mathsf{Z}_{t}
. Moreover, the covariance of
\mathsf{Z}_{t}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{s}
can be heuristically calculated based on a convenient property of OAMP algorithms. Specifically, for
{G}_{1}(\bm{W}),{G}_{2}(\bm{W})\in\big\{\widehat{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}),{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}),{\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W})\big\}
and
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2}\in\{\bm{x}_{\star},{\bm{f}}_{s}^{\perp},{\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}\}
, we have

\frac{\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}{G}_{1}(\bm{W}){G}_{2}(\bm{W})\bm{v}_{2}}{N}\approx\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[{G}_{1}(\bm{W}){G}_{2}(\bm{W})]}{N}\cdot\frac{\left\langle{\bm{v}_{1}},{\bm{v}_{2}}\right\rangle}{N}.

(29)

The intuition for the above property is that the random vectors
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2}
behave as if they are independent of the noise matrix
\bm{W}
, and the divergence-free and trace-free requirements imposed on OAMP algorithms are crucial for the validity of the above approximation. Using the above property, we immediately have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{s}\mathsf{Z}_{t}]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\left\langle{\bm{z}_{s}},{\bm{z}_{t}}\right\rangle}{N}=\frac{\left\langle{\alpha_{t}\widehat{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\bm{x}_{\star}+{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}){\bm{f}}_{t}^{\perp}},{\alpha_{s}\widehat{\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W})\bm{x}_{\star}+{\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W}){\bm{f}}_{s}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}{N}

(30)

\displaystyle\approx\alpha_{s}\alpha_{t}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\widehat{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\widehat{\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W})]}{N}\cdot\frac{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}+\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}){\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W})]}{N}\cdot\frac{\left\langle{\bm{f}_{t}^{\perp}},{\bm{f}_{s}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}{N},

where the cross terms vanish due to the orthogonality of
\bm{x}_{\star}
and
\bm{f}_{t}^{\perp}
(and
\bm{f}_{s}^{\perp}
). The limiting value of
\mathrm{Tr}[\widehat{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W}),\widehat{\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W})]/{N}
can be expressed in terms of
\nu
(the spectral measure in the signal direction) using the argument from (
28
). Replacing the various normalized inner products by their limiting values eventually leads to the claimed state evolution (
13
).

4.2
Derivation of the Optimal OAMP Algorithm

Next, we present an intuitive derivation of the optimal OAMP algorithm introduced in (
17
). While this derivation does not show that this algorithm attains the smallest estimation error among all iterative algorithms (as claimed in Theorem
2
), it provides a simple and natural approach to derive the matrix denoisers and the iterate denoisers used by the algorithm.
For simplicity, we consider the class of simple memory-free OAMP algorithms in which the iterate denoiser
f_{t}
only depends on the most recent iterate (cf. (
12
)):

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}(\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a}),\quad\forall\;t\in\mathbb{N},

(31)

We will use a natural greedy heuristic to design the matrix and iterate denoisers. Specifically, we derive a good choice of
\Psi_{t}
and
f_{t}
, assuming we have already specified the iterate and matrix denoisers for the first
t-1
iterations. For any candidate
\Psi_{t}
and
f_{t}
, the distribution of the state evolution random variable
\mathsf{X}_{t}
associated with the
\bm{x}_{t}
is given by (see Definition
3
):

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad\mathsf{X}_{t}=\beta_{t}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\mathsf{Z}_{t},

(32a)

where
\mathsf{Z}_{t}
is a centered Gaussian random variable independent of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})
, and

\displaystyle\beta_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu}\big[{\Psi}_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})],\quad\alpha_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{t}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t}^{2}\cdot\mathrm{Var}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu}[{\Psi}_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]+\left(\mathbb{E}[f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})^{2}]-\alpha_{t}^{2}\right)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}[{\Psi}_{t}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})].

A natural design principle is to choose
f_{t},\Psi_{t}
to maximize the SNR
\omega_{t}
of the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{t}=\beta_{t}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\mathsf{Z}_{t};\mathsf{A})
, which is given by the squared cosine similarity between
\mathsf{X}_{t},\mathsf{X}_{\star}
:

\displaystyle\omega_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\mathsf{X}_{t}\right]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}^{2}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{t}^{2}]}=\frac{\beta_{t}^{2}}{\beta_{t}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{t}^{2}]}=\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}_{t}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]+\frac{\delta_{t}}{1-\delta_{t}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[{\Psi}_{t}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]},

(33)

where
\delta_{t}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}1-\left(\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]\right)^{2}/\mathbb{E}[f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})^{2}]
. We consider the two maximization problems over
\Psi_{t}
and
f_{t}
one-by-one.

Optimal choice of
\Psi_{t}

Choosing
\Psi_{t}
to maximize
\omega_{t}
is equivalent to solving the following optimization problem:

\underset{\Psi}{\max}\ \frac{\left(\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]+\rho^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[{\Psi}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[{\Psi}(\mathsf{\Lambda})\right]=0.

(34)

with the parameter
\rho\in(0,\infty)
fixed at
\rho=\delta_{t}^{-1}-1
(notice
\delta_{t}
does not depend on
\Psi_{t}
). We transform the optimization problem in (
34
) into a simple quadratic optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{\Psi}{\min}\ 1-\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})]+\rho^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[{\Psi}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[{\Psi}(\mathsf{\Lambda})\right]=0

(35a)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\underset{\Psi}{\min}\min_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\left(1-c\Psi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})\right)^{2}\right]+\rho^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[c^{2}{\Psi}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[c{\Psi}(\mathsf{\Lambda})\right]=0

(35b)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{=}\underset{\Psi}{\min}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\left(1-\Psi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})\right)^{2}\right]+\rho^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[{\Psi}^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[{\Psi}(\mathsf{\Lambda})\right]=0,

(35c)

where step (a) can be verified by solving the inner quadratic minimization problem over
c
explicitly, and step (b) is due to a change of variable
\Psi\mapsto c\Psi
; note that
c\Psi
also satisfies the trace-free constraint. Note that any minimizer of (
35c
) is also a maximizer of (
34
) since the objective function of (
34
) is invariant to a rescaling of
\Psi
. To solve this optimization problem, we consider the Lebesgue decomposition
\nu=\nu_{\parallel}+\nu_{\perp}
, where
\nu_{\perp}
is the singular part and
\nu_{\parallel}
is the absolutely continuous part. Recall the definition of
\phi
from (
5
) and let
S
denote the set:

\displaystyle S\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:\phi(\lambda)\neq 0\}\quad\text{ where }\quad\phi(\lambda)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}{(1-\pi\theta{H}_{\mu}(\lambda))^{2}+\pi^{2}\theta^{2}\mu^{2}(\lambda)}.

(36)

Notice that
\mu(S^{c})=0
. Lemma
1
(items (2-3)) shows that
\nu_{\perp}(S)=0
and the density of
\nu_{\parallel}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure is given by
\mu(\cdot)/\phi(\cdot)
where
\mu(\cdot)
is the density of
\mu
(recall Assumption
1
). Hence, the Lagrangian of the minimization problem (
35c
) is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\Psi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})-1|^{2}]+\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]-2\gamma\mathbb{E}[\Psi(\mathsf{\Lambda})]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\Psi({\lambda})-1|^{2}\;\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{\Psi^{2}({\lambda})}{\rho}-2\gamma\Psi(\lambda)\right)\mu(\lambda)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{}}}{=}\int_{S^{c}}(\Psi(\lambda)-1)^{2}\;\nu_{\perp}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda)+\int_{S}\left((\Psi(\lambda)-1)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\phi(\lambda)}+\frac{\Psi^{2}(\lambda)}{\rho}-2\gamma\Psi(\lambda)\right)\;\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda),

where
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
is the Lagrange multiplier. The Lagrangian is minimized by minimizing the integrand pointwise, leading to the following formula for the minimizer
\Psi_{\gamma}
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\gamma}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\gamma+\frac{1}{\phi(\lambda)}}{\frac{1}{\phi(\lambda)}+\frac{1}{\rho}}=1+\frac{(\rho\gamma-1)\cdot\phi(\lambda)}{\rho+\phi(\lambda)}\quad\forall\;\lambda\;\in\;S\text{ and}\quad\Psi_{\gamma}(\lambda)=1\quad\forall\;\lambda\;\in\;S^{c}.

The solution of the constrained minimization problem (
35c
) is obtained by choosing
\gamma
so that the constraint
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}[\Psi_{\gamma}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]=0
is fulfilled. Hence, the optimizer of (
35c
) and (
34
) is:

\displaystyle\Psi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=1-\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}\left[\frac{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+\rho}\right]\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\phi(\lambda)+\rho}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\Psi_{\star}(\lambda;\rho)\quad\forall\;\lambda\;\in\;\mathbb{R}.

(37)

This is precisely the matrix denoiser used by the optimal OAMP algorithm in (
17
).

Optimal choice of
f_{t}

Notice from (
33
) that the iterate denoiser
f_{t}
which maximizes the SNR
\omega_{t}
can be derived by minimizing
\delta_{t}
with respect to
f_{t}
, leading to the minimization problem:

\underset{f}{\min}\ 1-\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]=0.

(38)

In the above definition,
\mathsf{X}_{t-1}=\beta_{t-1}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sigma_{t-1}\mathsf{Z}
is the state evolution random variable for
\bm{x}_{t-1}
,
\mathsf{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is independent of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})\sim\pi
, and
\beta_{t-1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma_{t-1}>0
are fixed parameters (determined by the matrix and iterate denoisers used in the first
t-1
iterations). Repeating the arguments used in (
35
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\min_{f}\ 1-\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]=0

(39a)

\displaystyle=\min_{f}\,\min_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathsf{X}_{\star}-cf(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})\right)^{2}\right]\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}[cf^{\prime}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]=0

(39b)

\displaystyle=\min_{f}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})\right)^{2}\right]\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}(\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})]=0

(39c)

Recalling the definition of the DMMSE estimator from Definition
2
(see also Lemma
3
in Appendix
A.2
), the minimizer of (
39c
) is:

f_{t}(x;a)=\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}\left((\beta_{t-1}^{2}+\sigma_{t-1}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot{x}{};a\big|\omega_{t-1}\right)\quad\text{where}\quad\omega_{t-1}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\beta_{t-1}^{2}}{\beta_{t-1}^{2}+\sigma_{t-1}^{2}}.

(40)

Optimal OAMP algorithm

Using the matrix and iterate denoisers derived above, we obtain the following memory-free OAMP algorithm (cf. (
31
)):

\bm{x}_{t}=c_{t}\cdot{\Psi}_{\star}(\bm{Y};\rho_{t})\cdot\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a}|\omega_{t-1}),

(41)

where we defined
c_{t}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}1/\sqrt{\beta_{t}^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}}
and made a slight change of variable by absorbing the normalization factor (acting on the
x
input of
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}
) in (
40
) into the definition of
\bm{x}_{t}
. This scaling parameter
c_{t}
ensures state evolution random variable
\mathsf{X}_{t}
corresponding to
\bm{x}_{t}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{t}^{2}]=1
. The resulting algorithm is precisely the optimal OAMP algorithm introduced in (
17
). The recursions for
\omega_{t},\rho_{t}
and the alternative formula
c_{t}=1/\sqrt{w_{t}}\cdot(1+1/\rho_{t})
is derived by specializing the general state evolution equations in (
32
) to the optimal OAMP algorithm; see the proof of Proposition
1
in Appendix
C
for details.

4.3
Proof of the Optimality Result (Theorem
2
)

We now present the proof of Theorem
2
. We will introduce the key ideas involved in the form of some intermediate results, whose proofs are deferred to Appendix
D
.

Implementing Iterative Algorithms using OAMP Algorithms

Our proof builds on the techniques introduced by
Celentano et al. [16]
and
Montanari and Wu [56]
, who show that for the i.i.d. Gaussian noise model, any estimator computed using
t\in\mathbb{N}
iterations of an iterative algorithm can also be computed using
t
iterations of a suitably designed AMP algorithm. Consequently, we can restrict the search space for the optimal
t
-iteration iterative algorithm to
t
-iteration AMP algorithms, which are easier to analyze. Based on these results, it is natural to hope that any estimator computed using
t
iterations of a general iterative algorithm (as defined in Definition
4
) can be computed using
t
iterations of a suitably designed OAMP algorithm (as defined in Definition
3
). Unfortunately, this strategy breaks down in our case because we require that the iterate denoisers used in the OAMP algorithm satisfy the divergence-free requirement (
15
) of Definition
3
, which is crucial to ensure that the OAMP algorithms have tractable state evolutions. We illustrate the difficulty caused by imposing the divergence-free requirement and motivate our proof strategy by trying to construct an OAMP algorithm to implement the first two iterations of a simple iterative algorithm:

\displaystyle\bm{r}_{1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{1}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{1}(\bm{a}),\quad\bm{r}_{2}=\Psi_{2}(\bm{Y})f_{2}(\bm{r}_{1};\bm{a}),

(42)

which we refer to as the
target algorithm
. Our goal is to design an OAMP algorithm which can reconstruct the iterates of the target algorithm. For simplicity, we assume that the matrix denoisers used by the target algorithm satisfy the trace-free requirement
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}[\Psi_{1}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}[\Psi_{2}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]=0
(requirement (
14
) in Definition
3
). Since the divergence-free constraint (
15
) is inactive for the first iteration, we can construct a valid OAMP algorithm which simply copies the first iteration of the target algorithm
\bm{x}_{1}=\Psi_{1}(\bm{Y})f_{1}(\bm{a})
. Let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})
be the state evolution random variables corresponding to the first iteration of this OAMP algorithm. Next, we consider the second iteration of the target algorithm (
42
). Notice that the denoiser
f_{2}
might not satisfy the divergence-free requirement
\mathbb{E}[\partial_{1}f_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]=0
imposed in the definition of OAMP algorithms (Definition
3
). To ensure this requirement, we design the second iteration of the OAMP algorithm as:

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{2}(\bm{Y})\cdot\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{1};\bm{a})\ \text{ where }\ \bm{\bar{}}{f}_{2}(x;a)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}{f}_{2}\left(x;a\right)-\mathbb{E}[\partial_{1}{f}_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]\cdot x\quad\forall\;x\in\mathbb{R},a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}.

By construction,
\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{2}
satisfies the divergence-free constraint
\mathbb{E}[\partial_{1}\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]=0
. We can express the second iterate of the target algorithm (
42
) as:

\displaystyle\bm{r}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bm{x}_{2}+\mathbb{E}[\partial_{1}{f}_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]\cdot\Psi_{2}(\bm{Y})\bm{x}_{1}\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\bm{x}_{2}+\mathbb{E}[\partial_{1}{f}_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]\cdot\underbrace{\Psi_{2}(\bm{Y})\Psi_{1}(\bm{Y})f_{1}(\bm{a})}_{\text{uncomputed}},

(43)

where in step (a) we recalled that
\bm{x}_{1}=\Psi_{1}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{1}(\bm{a})
. In particular, we are unable to reconstruct the second iterate of the target algorithm in (
42
) using the first two iterations of the OAMP algorithm we have designed since the matrix-vector product
\Psi_{2}(\bm{Y})\Psi_{1}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{1}(\bm{a})
in (
43
) has not been computed by the OAMP algorithm in the sense that it cannot be reconstructed from the OAMP iterates
\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}
.

Lifted OAMP Algorithms

To address the issue highlighted above, we introduce a more powerful class of algorithms called
lifted OAMP algorithms
, which are parameterized by a degree parameter
D\in\mathbb{N}
, which modulates the computational power of these algorithms. A degree-
D
OAMP algorithm can compute
D
matrix-vector products per iteration. For instance, in the first iteration, the algorithm computes the matrix-vector products (
{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}
):

\displaystyle(\bm{Y}-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}]\cdot\bm{I}_{N})f_{1}(\bm{a}),\;(\bm{Y}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}]\cdot\bm{I}_{N})f_{1}(\bm{a}),\;\dotsc,\;(\bm{Y}^{D}-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{D}]\cdot\bm{I}_{N})f_{1}(\bm{a}).

(44)

Since continuous functions can be approximated arbitrarily well by polynomial functions on compact sets, for any continuous function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we can approximate the matrix-vector product
\Phi(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{1}(\bm{a})
using a linear combination of the matrix-vector products in (
44
). In particular, the missing vector-matrix product in (
43
) needed to reconstruct the second iteration of the target algorithm (
42
) can also be approximated using the matrix-vector products (
44
) computed by the lifted OAMP algorithm. The following definition formally introduces the class of lifted OAMP algorithms.

Definition 5

(Degree-
D
Lifted OAMP Algorithm)
.

For any
D\in\mathbb{N}
(independent of
N
), a degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm maintains a sequence of iterates
({\bm{w}}_{ti})_{t\in\mathbb{N},i\in[D]}
indexed by a time index
t\in\mathbb{N}
and a degree index
i\in[D]
. At step
t
