, the lifted OAMP computes the iterates
{\bm{w}}_{t,1},\dotsc,{\bm{w}}_{t,D}
as follows:

\displaystyle{\bm{w}}_{t,i}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{Y}^{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}]\cdot\bm{I}_{N}\right)\cdot f_{t}\big({\bm{w}}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,{\bm{w}}_{t-1,\bullet};\bm{a}\big)\quad\forall\;i\in[D],\;t\in\mathbb{N}.

(45)

The estimate of
\bm{x}_{\star}
at iteration
t
is obtained by applying a post-processing function
\psi_{t}
to the iterates
\bm{w}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\bm{w}_{t,\bullet}
generated so far and the side-information
\bm{a}
:

\displaystyle{\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{t}({\bm{w}}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,{\bm{w}}_{t-1,\bullet};\bm{a}),

(46)

In the above equations, the notation
{\bm{w}}_{t,\bullet}
is a shorthand for the collection of vectors
{\bm{w}}_{t,1},{\bm{w}}_{t,2},\dotsc,{\bm{w}}_{t,D}
. Furthermore for each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the iterate denoiser
f_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{(t-1)D}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and the postprocessing function
\psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{tD}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
are continuously differentiable, Lipschitz, act entry-wise on their vector inputs, and do not change with
N
. Each lifted OAMP algorithm is associated with a collection of
state evolution random variables

(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{W}_{t,i})_{t\in\mathbb{N},i\in[D]};\mathsf{A})
, whose distribution is given by:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad\mathsf{W}_{t,i}=\alpha_{t}\cdot(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{\nu}\sim\nu}[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{\nu}^{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}^{i}])\cdot\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\mathsf{Z}_{ti}\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},i\;\in\;[D],

(47a)

where
\{\mathsf{Z}_{ti}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N},i\in\mathbb{N}}
are centered Gaussian random variables, sampled independently of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A}
) with covariance:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{si}\mathsf{Z}_{tj}]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{s}\alpha_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu}}}[\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}^{i},\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}^{j}]+(\Sigma_{st}-\alpha_{s}\alpha_{t})\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}}}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i},\mathsf{\Lambda}^{j}].

(47b)

In the above equations,
(\alpha_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\Sigma_{st})_{s\in\mathbb{N},t\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by the recursion:

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{X}_{\star}{f}_{t}\left(\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t-1,\bullet};\mathsf{A}\right)\right],\ \Sigma_{st}=\mathbb{E}\left[{f}_{s}\left(\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{s-1,\bullet};\mathsf{A}\right){f}_{t}\left(\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t-1,\bullet};\mathsf{A}\right)\right].

(47c)

Finally, we require the iterate denoisers
(f_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
to satisfy the
divergence-free
constraint:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\partial_{s,i}f_{t}(\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t-1,\bullet};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle=0\quad\forall\;s\;\in\;[t-1],\;i\;\in\;[D],\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},

(48)

where
\partial_{s,i}f_{t}(w_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,w_{t-1,\bullet};a)
denotes the partial derivative of
f_{t}
with respect to
w_{s,i}
.

A degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm which runs for
t
iterations can be viewed as an instance of an OAMP algorithm (Definition
3
) which runs for
tD
iterations. Hence, the following result as an immediate corollary of Theorem
1
.

Corollary 1

(State evolution of lifted OAMP algorithms)
.

Consider a degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm of the form (
45
) and let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{W}_{t,i})_{t\in\mathbb{N},i\in[D]};\mathsf{A})
be the associated state evolution random variables. Then for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left(\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{w}_{1,\bullet},\bm{w}_{2,\bullet},\dotsc,\bm{w}_{t,\bullet};\bm{a}\right)\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A}).

The following proposition (proved in Appendix
D.1
), shows that any estimator that is computed by an iterative algorithm (Definition
4
) can be approximated arbitrarily well by a suitably designed degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm in the limit
D\rightarrow\infty
.

Proposition 3
.

Let
\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}
be the estimator returned by any iterative algorithm (Definition
4
) after
t\in\mathbb{N}
iterations. Then, for each
D\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm which returns an estimator
\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}
after
t
iterations, which satisfies:

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\plimsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}-\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=0.

The Optimal Lifted OAMP Algorithm

Since Proposition
3
guarantees that any estimator computed using an iterative algorithm can be approximated by a suitably designed lifted OAMP algorithm, we focus on characterizing the optimal lifted OAMP algorithms. The optimal degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm takes the form:

\displaystyle{\bm{w}}_{t,i}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{Y}^{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}]\cdot\bm{I}_{N}\right)\cdot f_{t}^{\star}\big({\bm{w}}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,{\bm{w}}_{t-1,\bullet};\bm{a}\big)\quad\forall\;i\in[D],\;t\in\mathbb{N},

(49a)

and returns the following estimator after
t\in\mathbb{N}
iterations:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}

\displaystyle=h_{t}^{\star}({\bm{w}}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,{\bm{w}}_{t-1,\bullet};\bm{a}).

(49b)

The description of the iterate denoisers
(f_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and the post-processing functions
(h_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
used by the optimal lifted OAMP algorithm is recursive. Suppose that the functions
f^{\star}_{1},\dotsc,f_{t}^{\star}
have been specified. Let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})
denote the state evolution random variables for the first
t
iterations of the resulting lifted OAMP algorithm. Then:

1.

The post-processing function
h_{t}^{\star}
used at iteration
t
is the MMSE estimator (recall Definition
2
) for the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})
.

2.

f_{t+1}^{\star}
, the iterate denoiser used by the lifted OAMP algorithm in the next iteration, is the DMMSE estimator for the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})
.

The following proposition, shows that the estimator (
49
) achieves the lowest mean squared error among all estimators that can be computed using
t
iterations of a degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm.

Proposition 4
.

Let
\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}
be the estimator described in (
49
). Let
\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}
be any other estimator that can be computed using
t
iterations of some degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm. Then, we have:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\leq\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}.

Proof Sketch.

We outline the main ideas here and defer the complete proof to Appendix
D.2
. Consider a general lifted OAMP algorithm:

\displaystyle{\bm{w}}_{t,i}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{Y}^{i}-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}]\cdot\bm{I}_{N}\right)\cdot f_{t}\big({\bm{w}}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,{\bm{w}}_{t-1,\bullet};\bm{a}\big)\quad\forall\;i\in[D],\;t\in\mathbb{N},

which returns the estimator
\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}=h_{t}(\bm{w}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\bm{w}_{t,\bullet};\bm{a})
after
t
iterations. By Corollary
1
, the asymptotic mean squared error for this estimator is
\mathbb{E}|\mathsf{X}_{\star}-h_{t}(\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})|^{2}
, where
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})
denote the state evolution random variables associated with the lifted OAMP algorithm. Recalling the definition of the MMSE denoiser for a Gaussian channel from Definition
2
, we find that the optimal choice for the post-processing function is
h_{t}=h_{t}^{\star}
, where
h_{t}^{\star}
is the MMSE estimator for the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})
. With this optimal choice of the post-processing function, the limiting MSE of the resulting estimator is
\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})
, which depends implicitly on the iterate denoisers
f_{1:t}
used in the lifted OAMP algorithm since
f_{1:t}
determine the joint distribution of the state evolution random variables. To make this dependence explicit, we define
{M}_{t}(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{W}_{1,\bullet},\dotsc,\mathsf{W}_{t,\bullet};\mathsf{A})
for each
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Deriving the optimal choice of the iterate denoisers is equivalent to minimizing the functional
{M}_{t}(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t})
with respect to
f_{1:t}
. The crux of the argument is to show that the iterate denoisers
(f_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
used by the optimal lifted OAMP algorithm (
49
) correspond to a
greedy approach
to minimizing
{M}_{t}
:

\displaystyle f_{1}^{\star}\in\argmin_{f_{1}}{M}_{1}(f_{1}),\ f_{2}^{\star}\in\argmin_{f_{2}}{M}_{2}(f_{1}^{\star},f_{2}),\ \dotsc\quad f_{t}^{\star}\in\argmin_{f_{t}}{M}_{t}(f_{1:t-1}^{\star},f_{t}).

(50)

Moreover, we show that the functional
{M}_{t}
has the property that for any choice of iterate denoisers
f_{1:t-1}
:

\displaystyle\min_{f_{t}}{M}_{t}(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t})

\displaystyle={A}({M}_{t-1}(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t-1})),

(51)

for some explicit
non-decreasing
function
{A}:[0,1]\mapsto[0,1]
. This property guarantees that the greedy approach (
50
) finds the global minimizer of
{M}_{t}
. Indeed, if we assume as our induction hypothesis that
{M}_{t-1}(f_{1:t-1}^{\star})\leq{M}_{t-1}(f_{1:t-1})
for any choice of
f_{1:t-1}
, we can conclude that:

\displaystyle{M}_{t}(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{f_{t}}{M}_{t}(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t})\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:key-prop}}}}{=}{A}({M}_{t-1}(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{t-1}))

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{\geq}{A}({M}_{t-1}(f_{1}^{\star},\dotsc,f_{t-1}^{\star}))\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:key-prop}}}}{=}\min_{f_{t}}{M}_{t}(f_{1}^{\star},\dotsc,f_{t-1}^{\star},f_{t})\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:greedy-approach-crux}}}}{=}{M}_{t}(f_{1}^{\star},\dotsc,f_{t}^{\star}),

where the inequality (a) follows from the induction hypothesis and the monotonicity of
{A}
.
∎

Simplifying the optimal lifted OAMP algorithm

Observe that the optimal degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm introduced in (
49
) is not an iterative algorithm in the sense of Definition
4
. Indeed, an iterative algorithm is allowed a single matrix-vector multiplication per iteration, whereas the optimal lifted OAMP computes
D
matrix-vector products per iteration. Fortunately, the optimal lifted OAMP algorithm (
49
) can be simplified to eliminate these extra matrix-vector multiplications. This simplification leads to the following result, which shows that the optimal OAMP algorithm introduced in (
17
) can match the infinite degree (
D\rightarrow\infty
) limit of the performance of the optimal lifted OAMP algorithm in (
49
).

Proposition 5
.

Let
{\widehat{\bm{x}}}_{t}
be the estimator returned by the optimal OAMP algorithm in (
17
) after
t
iterations. Let
\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}
be the estimator returned by optimal degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm in (
49
) after
t
iterations. Then, for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|{\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\;\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}.

Proof Sketch.

We outline the high-level idea here, a complete proof is given in Appendix
D.3
. We show that the estimator
\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}
returned by the optimal lifted OAMP algorithm (
49
) after
t
iterations depends
only
on a linear combination
\bm{x}_{t}^{(D)}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}v_{i}\cdot\bm{w}_{t,i}
of the iterates
\bm{w}_{t,\bullet}
generated at the last step of the algorithm. Thanks to this property, the
\bm{x}_{t}^{(D)}
(and hence, the estimator
\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}
) can be computed using a single (instead of
D
) matrix-vector multiplication(s):

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}^{(D)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}v_{i}\bm{w}_{t,i}^{(D)}\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:optimal-LOAMP}}}}{=}\bigg(\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{D}v_{i}(\bm{Y}^{i}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu}[\mathsf{\Lambda^{i}}]\cdot\bm{I}_{N})}_{\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\Psi_{t}^{(D)}(\bm{Y})}\bigg)f_{t}^{\star}(\bm{w}_{<t,\bullet};\bm{a})=\Psi_{t}^{(D)}(\bm{Y})f_{t}^{\star}(\bm{w}_{<t,\bullet};\bm{a}).

We argue that as
D\rightarrow\infty
the matrix denoiser
\Psi_{t}^{(D)}
that appears in the above equation converges to the optimal matrix denoiser used by the optimal OAMP algorithm (
17
) in an
L^{2}
sense, and hence the claimed result follows.
∎

The optimality result stated as Theorem
2
is an immediate consequence of the above intermediate propositions.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Let
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}
be the estimator returned by the optimal OAMP algorithm from (
17
) and let
\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}
be any estimator that can be computed using
t
iterations of some iterative algorithm. Proposition
3
guarantees the existence of a sequence of estimators
(\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)})_{D\in\mathbb{N}}
that approximate
\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}
and can be computed using
t
iterations of a degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm. Finally, let
\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}
be the estimator returned by the optimal degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm from (
49
). By the reverse triangle inequality, for any
D\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|\geq\|\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|-\|\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}\|
. We let
N\rightarrow\infty
and then
D\rightarrow\infty
to obtain:

\displaystyle\pliminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|}{\sqrt{N}}\overset{\text{\tiny{}}}{\geq}\liminf_{D\rightarrow\infty}\pliminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|}{\sqrt{N}}-\limsup_{D\rightarrow\infty}\plimsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\widehat{\bm{r}}_{t}\|}{\sqrt{N}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{Prop. \ref{prop:LOAMP-reduction}}}}{=}\;\;\liminf_{D\rightarrow\infty}\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|}{\sqrt{N}}\;\;\overset{\text{\tiny{Prop. \ref{prop:greedy-optimality}}}}{\geq}\;\;\liminf_{D\rightarrow\infty}\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{t}^{(D)}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|}{\sqrt{N}}\;\;\overset{\text{\tiny{Prop. \ref{prop:simplification}}}}{=}\;\;\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|{\widehat{\bm{x}}_{t}}-\bm{x}_{\star}\|}{\sqrt{N}}.

Comparing the first and final expressions in the above display proves the theorem.
∎

5
Numerical Experiments

We conclude the paper with some numerical experiments. Appendix
F
provides additional numerical results.

Following
[
50
,
69
]
, we generate the noise matrix
\bm{W}
in a structured manner, making it feasible to efficiently simulate the dynamics of iterative algorithms on very high-dimensional matrices (
N=2\times 10^{6}
). Specifically, we use the noise matrix
\bm{W}=\bm{O}\text{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})\bm{O}^{\mathsf{T}}
with
\bm{O}=\bm{S}_{1}\bm{F}\bm{S}_{2}\bm{F}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{S}_{3}
, where
\bm{S}_{1},\bm{S}_{2},\bm{S}_{3}
are independent random signed diagonal matrices, and
\bm{F}
is the discrete cosine transform matrix. The eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
are sampled independently from a spectral
\mu
. We remark that known universality results
[
25
,
77
,
24
]
guarantee that using this structured noise matrix (instead of random rotationally invariant noise drawn from the quartic model) does not change the asymptotic dynamics of AMP algorithms. The signal
\bm{x}_{\star}
is sampled from an i.i.d. two-point prior
\epsilon^{2}\delta_{1/\epsilon}+(1-\epsilon^{2})\delta_{0}
, where
0<\epsilon<1
.

(a)
Statistical-computational gap exists.

(b)
No statistical-computational gap.

Figure 2:
Performance (measured by the normalized overlap with signal) of PCA, the Bayes estimator, and the optimal OAMP algorithm in (
17
). (a) The spectral
\mu
corresponds to the quartic noise model in (
21
) with parameter
\gamma=0
. The signal
\bm{x}_{\star}
is sampled from an i.i.d. two-point prior
\epsilon^{2}\delta_{1/\epsilon}+(1-\epsilon^{2})\delta_{0}
, where
\epsilon=1/8
. (b) The spectral
\mu
corresponds to the purely sestic noise model (see (
240a
) in the appendix) and the the signal is sampled from the two-point prior with
\epsilon=1/\sqrt{5}
.

Figs.
2a
and
2b
compare the performance of the optimal OAMP algorithm with the state evolution prediction from Theorem
1
. In our simulations, the OAMP algorithm is initialized by
f_{1}(\bm{x}_{0};\bm{a})=\epsilon\cdot\bm{1}
(which is the mean of the two-point prior) and no side information is provided. We also plot the performance of PCA and the Bayes-optimal estimator. In both Fig.
2a
and Fig.
2b
, OAMP achieves much better estimation accuracy than the PCA estimator and matches the Bayes-optimal performance at high SNR. Moreover, the performance of OAMP closely matches its state evolution. Notice that, for Fig.
2a
, there exists a performance gap between the conjectured Bayes-optimal performance (as predicted by the replica method) and the performance achieved by optimal OAMP (which has been shown to be optimal among a broad class of iterative algorithms). On the other hand, no such statistical-computational gap exists for Fig.
2b
where the signal is relatively “dense” (namely, the fraction of the nonzero components is relatively large). Similar phenomena have been observed in the context of spiked Wigner models
[
20
,
54
]
.

The theoretical prediction of the asymptotic performance of OAMP requires the noise matrix to be rotationally-invaraint. The noise matrix used in the experiments for Fig.
2
is structured but still synthetic. We now consider a more realistic model where the noise matrix is obtained from real datasets (while the signal is still randomly generated). Taking inspiration from
[
78
]
, we generate the noise matrix from the covariance matrix of real datasets in bioinformatics, namely, the 1000 Genomes Project (1000G)
[
18
]
and International HapMap
Project (Hapmap3)
[
19
]
. Both datasets undergo the preprocessing steps, producing a
2054\times 2054
symmetric matrix for 1000G and a
1397\times 1397
symmetric matrix for Hapmap3; see Appendix
F
for details of the data processing procedure.

(a)
Noise matrix derived from 1000G
[
18
]
.

(b)
Noise matrix derived from Hapmap3
[
19
]
.

Figure 3:
Performance of a data-driven implementation of the optimal degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm on noise matrices derived from real datasets and signals randomly drawn from
\mathsf{X}_{\star}\sim 0.1\mathcal{N}(0,10)+0.9\delta_{0}
. The results are averaged over 30 random realizations.

Running the optimal OAMP algorithm requires estimating the spectral measure
\mu
and the associated optimal matrix denoiser (
17e
) from the observed data.
While feasible, we found it more convenient to design a data-driven implementation of the optimal degree-
D
lifted OAMP algorithm introduced in (
49
). Fig.
3
also displays the asymptotic MSE predicted by the state evolution result for the corresponding rotationally invariant noise model with a matching spectrum. The performance of the lifted OAMP algorithm closely aligns with the state evolution prediction, suggesting an underlying universality phenomenon.

6
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper, we introduced an OAMP algorithm for spiked matrix models with rotationally-invariant noise and demonstrated that it obeys a simple state evolution characterization in the high-dimensional limit. We showed that the optimal OAMP algorithm achieves the best performance among a broad class of iterative algorithms.

Several promising directions for future research remain. As a first step, our analysis focused on a stylized symmetric rank-one model, and it would be valuable to extend these results to more practical settings, including asymmetric and multi-rank models. As discussed in Remark 1, in the absence of side information, if the signal is drawn from a zero-mean prior, no iterative algorithm that runs for a constant (
N
-independent) number of iterations can achieve non-trivial estimation error. An interesting direction for future work is to analyze OAMP algorithms with spectral initialization
[
76
,
53
,
79
]
, or randomly initialized iterative algorithms that run for a diverging (
N
-dependent) number of iterations
[
70
,
46
,
47
]
. Finally, since the optimal OAMP algorithm relies on the knowledge of the signal prior and noise spectrum, it would be interesting to develop and analyze a practical procedure to estimate these parameters from the data
[
78
]
.

Appendix A
Preliminary Results

A.1
Random Matrix Theory

This appendix collects some useful random matrix theory results. We present the proof of Lemma
1
and a useful result on the concentration of quadratic forms involving rotationally invariant matrices (Fact
1
).

A.1.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Proof of Lemma
1
.

Before presenting the proof of Lemma
1
, we recall the definition of the Stieltjes transform of a probability measure. For any probability measure (or more generally, finite measure)
\chi
on
\mathbb{R}
, the Stieltjes Transform
{S}_{\chi}:\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{C}
of
\chi
is defined as:

\displaystyle{S}_{\chi}(z)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\chi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda)}{z-\lambda}\quad\forall\;z\;\in\;\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}.

It is well-known that a probability measure is uniquely determined by its Stieltjes transform (see for e.g.,
[
2
, Theorem 2.4.3]
). We prove each of the claims made in Lemma
1
one by one.

Weak convergence of
\nu_{N}
(Claim (1))

Let
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}
be a slightly modified version of
\nu_{N}
:

\widetilde{\nu}_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}_{i}\right)^{2}\cdot\delta_{\lambda_{i}(\bm{Y})}

where
\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{\star}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\sqrt{N}\bm{x}_{\star}}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|}
. This modification makes
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}
a probability measure, i.e.,
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}(\mathbb{R})=1
. Notice that for any bounded, continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\lambda)\;\widetilde{\nu}_{N}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\lambda)\;{\nu}_{N}(\lambda).

By Assumption
1
,
\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}/N\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}1
. Hence, it suffices to show that
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}
converges weakly to a probability measure
\nu
in probability. To do so, it suffices to verify that the Stieltjes transform
{S}_{\widetilde{\nu}_{N}}(z)
of
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}
converges pointwise to the Stieltjes transform of a probability measure
\nu
for any
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}
(see for e.g.,
[
2
, Theorem 2.4.4]
). To this end, we note that:

\displaystyle{S}_{\widetilde{\nu}_{N}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\left\langle{\bm{u}_{i}(\bm{Y})},{\bm{x}_{\star}}\right\rangle^{2}}{z-\lambda_{i}(\bm{Y})}=\frac{1}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\bm{Y}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star},

where
\lambda_{1:N}(\bm{Y})
and
\bm{u}_{1:N}(\bm{Y})
denote the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
\bm{Y}
. Hence,

\displaystyle{S}_{\widetilde{\nu}_{N}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\bm{Y}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}=\frac{1}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\frac{\theta\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}}{N}-\bm{W}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}

(52)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\frac{\frac{1}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\bm{W}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}}{1-\frac{\theta}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\bm{W}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}},

(53)

where step (a) uses the Sherman-Morrison formula for the rank-one update to the matrix inverse. Using standard concentration results regarding quadratic forms of rotationally invariant matrices (stated as Fact
1
in Appendix
A.1.2
for convenience), we find that:

\displaystyle\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\bm{W}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda)}{z-\lambda}={S}_{\mu}(z)\quad\forall\;z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}.

(54)

By Assumption
1
,
\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}/N\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}1
. Hence,

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\theta}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\bm{W}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}

\displaystyle=1-\theta\cdot\frac{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}\cdot\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(z\bm{I}_{N}-\bm{W}\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}}{\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}

(55)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}1-\theta{S}_{\mu}(z)\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{\neq}0\quad\forall\;z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R},

(56)

where the non-equality marked (a) follows by observing that
\Im({S}_{\mu}(z))\neq 0
whenever
\mathrm{Im}(z)\neq 0
, and so
1-\theta{S}_{\mu}(z)\neq 0
. Plugging (
54
) and (
55
) into (
52
), we obtain:

{S}_{\widetilde{\nu}_{N}}(z)\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{{S}_{\mu}(z)}{1-\theta{S}_{\mu}(z)}\quad\forall\;z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}.

(57)

To prove the weak convergence of
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}
to a
probability measure

\nu
, we need to show additionally that the RHS of the above equation is the Stieltjes transform of some probability measure. By
[
51
, Theorem 10, Chapter 3]
, it is sufficient to verify that:

\displaystyle\limsup_{y\to\infty}y\left|\frac{{S}_{\mu}(\imath y)}{1-\theta{S}_{\mu}(\imath y)}\right|=1.

(58)

Since
\mu
is a probability measure, we have
y|{S}_{\mu}(\imath y)|\to 1
and
{S}_{\mu}(\imath y)\to 0
as
y\to\infty
(see
[
51
, Lemma 3, Chapter 3]
). Hence, (
58
) holds. Consequently, the RHS of (
57
) is a Stieltjes transform of a probability measure
\nu
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}
and
\nu_{N}
converge weakly to
\nu
in probability.

Lebesgue Decomposition of
\nu
(Claims (2) and (3))

Belinschi [11, Lemma 2.17]
has shown that for any probability measure
\chi
on
\mathbb{R}
with Lebesgue decomposition
\chi=\chi_{\parallel}+\chi_{\perp}
, the Stieltjes transform of
\nu
satisfies:

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\Im[{S}_{\chi}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]

\displaystyle=-\infty\quad\text{for $\chi_{\perp}$-almost every $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$},

(59)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\Im[{S}_{\chi}(x+\imath\epsilon)]

\displaystyle=-\pi\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\chi_{\parallel}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}(\lambda)\quad\text{for Lebesgue-almost every $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$}.

(60)

In the display above,
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\chi_{\parallel}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}
denotes the density of
\chi_{\parallel}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure and the limit on the LHS of (
60
) is guaranteed to exist and is finite for Lebesgue-almost every
\lambda
. In light of this result, we study the limit:

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\Im[{S}_{\nu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]

for an arbitrary
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. For any
\epsilon>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\Im[{S}_{\nu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]

\displaystyle=\Im\left[\frac{{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)}{1-\theta{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)}\right]=\frac{\Im[{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]}{(1-\theta\Re[{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)])^{2}+\theta^{2}\Im^{2}[{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]}.

Since
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure (Assumption
1
), (
60
) implies:

\displaystyle\Im[{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]\rightarrow-\pi\mu(\lambda)\quad\text{ as }\epsilon\downarrow 0.

where
\mu(\cdot)
denotes the density of
\mu
. Moreover, by the Sohotsky-Plemelj formula (see for instance,
[
61
, Section 2.1]
),

\displaystyle\Re[{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]\rightarrow\pi{H}_{\mu}(\lambda)\quad\text{ as }\epsilon\downarrow 0.

Recall that:

\displaystyle\phi(\lambda)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}(1-\theta\pi{H}_{\mu}(\lambda))^{2}+\theta^{2}\pi^{2}\cdot\mu^{2}(\lambda).

Hence,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\Im[{S}_{\nu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]

\displaystyle=-\infty\quad\text{ is possible only when }\phi(\lambda)=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\Im[{S}_{\nu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi\mu(\lambda)}{\phi(\lambda)}\in(-\infty,0]\quad\text{ iff }\phi(\lambda)\neq 0.

Combining the above results with (
59
) and (
60
), we conclude that:

\displaystyle\phi(\lambda)

\displaystyle\neq 0\quad\text{ for Lebesgue-almost every $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$.},

\displaystyle\phi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{ for $\nu_{\perp}$-almost every $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\parallel}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu(\lambda)}{\phi(\lambda)}\quad\text{ for Lebesgue-almost every $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$,}

as claimed. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

A.1.2
Concentration of Quadratic Forms

Fact 1

(
Benaych-Georges and Nadakuditi 12, Proposition 9.3
)
.

Let
\bm{W}
be a
N\times N
random noise matrix which satisfies Assumption
1
and let
\bm{u}
be a
N
-dimensional random vector which is independent of
\bm{W}
and satisfies:
\|\bm{u}\|^{2}/N\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}r
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Then, for any continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\bm{u}^{\top}f(\bm{W})\bm{u}}{N}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}r\cdot\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{\Lambda})]\quad\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu,

where
\mu
is the limiting spectral distribution of
\bm{W}
.

Proof.

Recall from Assumption
1
that the eigen-decomposition of
\bm{W}
is given by:

\displaystyle\bm{W}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}\cdot\diag(\lambda_{1}(\bm{W}),\dotsc,\lambda_{N}(\bm{W}))\cdot\bm{U}^{\top},

where the matrix of eigenvectors
\bm{U}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathbb{O}(N))
is a Haar-distributed random orthogonal matrix independent of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\bm{W}),\dotsc,\lambda_{N}(\bm{W})
.
Defining
\bm{v}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\bm{U}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}/\|\bm{u}\|\sim\text{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{N-1})
, we find that:

\displaystyle\frac{\bm{u}^{\top}f(\bm{W})\bm{u}}{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\bm{u}\|^{2}}{N}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}^{2}\cdot f(\lambda_{i}(\bm{W})).

Since
\bm{v}\sim\text{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{N-1})
, by
[
12
, Proposition 9.3]
we have that:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}^{2}\cdot f(\lambda_{i}(\bm{W}))\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{\Lambda})],\quad\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu.

The claim follows by combining the above conclusion with the hypothesis
\|\bm{u}\|^{2}/N\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}r
using Slutsky’s theorem.
∎

A.2
Gaussian Channels

We state and prove some important properties of Gaussian channels (Definition
2
) in this appendix.

The DMMSE Estimator for Scalar Gaussian Channels

The following lemma, due to
Ma and Ping [50]
provides a formula for the DMMSE estimator for a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\omega
. We provide a proof of this result in Section
A.2.1
for completeness.

Lemma 2

(
Ma and Ping [50]
)
.

Let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})
be a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\omega\in[0,1]
. Then,

1.

The function
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\cdot|\omega):\mathbb{R}^{1+k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
(
\forall\;x\in\mathbb{R},\;a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
):

\displaystyle\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(x;a|\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\begin{cases}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{\omega}}{\sqrt{1-\omega}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi({\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]}\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(\varphi(x;a|\omega)-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi({\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}}|\omega)]}{\sqrt{1-\omega}}\cdot x\right)&:\omega<1\\
x&:\omega=1\end{cases}

(61)

is the DMMSE estimator for
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})
.

2.

The DMMSE estimator satisfies the identities:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)^{2}]=1-\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega).

3.

When
\omega<1
, the
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}
and
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}
functions are related by the identity:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}-\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}.

MMSE and DMMSE Estimators for General Gaussian Channels

Next, we provide formulas for the MMSE and DMMSE estimators for general (multivariate) Gaussian channels. Consider a Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad(Z_{1},\dotsc,Z_{t})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Sigma\right),\quad\mathsf{X}_{i}=\alpha_{i}\cdot\mathsf{X}_{\star}+Z_{i}\quad\forall\;i\;\in\;[t],

where the Gaussian noise
(Z_{1},\dotsc,Z_{t})
is sampled independently of the signal and side information
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
. Observe that for any vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{t}
which satisfies:

\displaystyle\left\langle{v},{\alpha}\right\rangle^{2}+v^{\top}\Sigma v

\displaystyle=1,

(62)

(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\left\langle{v},{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A})
forms a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\omega=\left\langle{v},{\alpha}\right\rangle^{2}
. Among all vectors
v\in\mathbb{R}^{t}
which satisfy (
62
), the maximum SNR for the corresponding scalar channel is achieved by the vector:

\displaystyle v_{\mathrm{opt}}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\begin{cases}\left(\alpha^{\top}\Sigma^{\dagger}\alpha+(\alpha^{\top}\Sigma^{\dagger}\alpha)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Sigma^{\dagger}\alpha&\text{ if }\alpha\in\mathrm{Range}(\Sigma),\\
\left\langle{P_{\perp}[\alpha]},{\alpha}\right\rangle^{-1}\cdot P_{\perp}[\alpha]&\text{ if }\alpha\notin\mathrm{Range}(\Sigma),\end{cases}

(63a)

where
\Sigma^{\dagger}
denotes the pseudo-inverse of
\Sigma
,
\mathrm{Range}(\Sigma)
is the range space of
\Sigma
, and
P_{\perp}[\cdot]
denotes the projector on the orthogonal complement of
\mathrm{Range}(\Sigma)
. We will refer to
v_{\mathrm{opt}}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
as the
optimal linear combination
for the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
. The maximum SNR is given by:

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left\langle{\alpha},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})}\right\rangle^{2}

(63b)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(1+\alpha^{\top}\Sigma^{\dagger}\alpha\right)^{-1}\cdot\alpha^{\top}\Sigma^{\dagger}\alpha&\text{ if }\alpha\in\mathrm{Range}(\Sigma),\\
1&\text{ if }\alpha\notin\mathrm{Range}(\Sigma),\end{cases}

which we call the
effective SNR
of the Gaussian channel. The following lemma shows that the MMSE and DMMSE of the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
is same as the MMSE and DMMSE of the scalar Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\left\langle{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A})
, which operates at the SNR
\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}
.

Lemma 3
.

Let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
be a Gaussian channel. Let
v_{\mathrm{opt}}
and
\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}
denote the optimal linear combination and effective SNR for the Gaussian channel, as defined in (
63
). Then:

1.

\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t},\mathsf{A})=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})
and the function:

\displaystyle f_{\star}(x;a)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\varphi(\left\langle{x},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;a|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})\quad\forall\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{t},\quad a\in\mathbb{R}^{k},

is the MMSE estimator:

\displaystyle f_{\star}

\displaystyle\in\arg\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}].

2.

\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t},\mathsf{A})=\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})
and the function:

\displaystyle\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{\star}(x;a)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\left\langle{x},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;a|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})\quad\forall\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{t},\quad a\in\mathbb{R}^{k},

is the DMMSE estimator:

\displaystyle\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{\star}\in

\displaystyle\arg\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\text{subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})]=0\;\forall\;i\;\in\;[t].

Proof.

The proof is deferred to Section
A.2.2
.
∎

Monotonicity of MMSE and DMMSE

Lastly, we will rely on the following natural monotonicity property of the
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}
function.

Fact 2

(
Guo et al. 33, Proposition 9
)
.

The function
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}:[0,1]\mapsto[0,1]
is a continuous, non-increasing function. If
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(0)=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]]\neq 0
,
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}:[0,1]\mapsto[0,1]
is strictly decreasing.

The following lemma, whose proof is provided in Section
A.2.3
, shows that the
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}
function is also non-decreasing.

Lemma 4
.

The function
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}:[0,1]\mapsto[0,1]
is non-increasing. Moreover,
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\cdot)
is continuous on the domain
[0,1)
.

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proofs of Lemma
2
, Lemma
3
, and Lemma
4
introduced above.

A.2.1
Proof of Lemma
2

Proof of Lemma
2
.

This result is due to
Ma and Ping [50]
, we provide a proof here for completeness. We consider a scalar Gaussian channel at SNR
\omega
:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad\mathsf{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right),\quad\mathsf{X}=\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sqrt{1-\omega}\cdot\mathsf{Z},

(64)

where the Gaussian noise
\mathsf{Z}
is sampled independently of the signal and side information
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
. Recall from Definition
2
that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})

(65)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\quad\text{subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]=0.

Moreover, the function that achieves the lowest MSE in (
65
) is the DMMSE estimator for the channel. Let
f
be any function in
L^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})
which satisfies the divergence-free requirement
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]=0
. We can expand the mean squared error (MSE) of
f
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}))^{2}]

\displaystyle=1+\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]-2\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]

(66)

\displaystyle=1+\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]-2\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})],

where we used the tower property in the last step. For convenience, we define:

\displaystyle\beta\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]}{\sqrt{1-\omega}},\quad\widehat{\varphi}(x;a|\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\varphi(x;a|\omega)-\beta x.

(67)

Observe that
\widehat{\varphi}
satisfies the divergence-free requirement:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\widehat{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]

\displaystyle=0.

(68)

We consider the cross-term in (
66
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]+\beta\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]+\beta\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]+\beta\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})].

(69)

In the above display step (a) follows from the fact that
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]=0
and step (b) uses the tower property. We claim that:

\displaystyle\omega<1\implies\beta{\sqrt{\omega}}\neq 1.

(70)

Assuming the above claim, we can split our analysis into two cases.

Case 1:
\omega<1

We first prove each of the claims made in the lemma assuming
\omega<1
.

Proof of Claim 1.

Notice that (
70
) allows us to rearrange (
69
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]}{1-\beta\sqrt{\omega}}=\mathbb{E}[{\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})],

(71)

where we recalled from the statement of the lemma that
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}
was defined as:

\displaystyle\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(x;a|\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{\omega}}{\sqrt{1-\omega}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi({\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]}\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(\varphi(x;a|\omega)-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi({\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}}|\omega)]}{\sqrt{1-\omega}}\cdot x\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:hat-psi-def}}}}{=}\frac{\varphi(x;a|\omega)-\beta x}{1-\beta\sqrt{\omega}}\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:hat-psi-def}}}}{=}\frac{\widehat{\varphi}(x;a|\omega)}{1-\beta\sqrt{\omega}}.

(72)

Substituting the formula (
71
) in (
66
) and completing the square yields the following MSE decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}))^{2}]

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{E}[\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\{f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})-\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]

(73)

Since
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}
satisfies the divergence-free requirement
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]=0
(cf. (
68
)), the above decomposition shows that
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}
is the DMMSE estimator.

Proof of Claim 2.

Setting
f=\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\cdot\;;\cdot|\omega)
in (
71
) and (
73
) yields:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=1-\mathbb{E}[(\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega))^{2}]=1-\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega).

By the Tower property,
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=1-\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega),

(74)

which is the second claim made in the lemma.

Proof of Claim 3.

Notice from (
72
) that the MMSE and DMMSE estimators are related by:

\displaystyle\varphi(x;a|\omega)=(1-\beta\sqrt{\omega})\cdot\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(x;a|\omega)+\beta x.

(75)

Hence, we can relate
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)
and
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)=\mathbb{E}[(\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega))^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}^{2}]-2\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]+\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}1-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]

(76)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:mmse-dmmse-denoiser-connection}}}}{=}1-(1-\beta\sqrt{\omega})\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]-\beta\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\mathsf{X}]

\displaystyle=(1-\beta\sqrt{\omega})\cdot(1-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)])

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:scalar-dmmse-claim2}}}}{=}(1-\beta\sqrt{\omega})\cdot\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega).

(77)

In the above display, step (a) relies on the assumption
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}^{2}]=1
(cf. Assumption
1
) and the tower property
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)^{2}]
.
Moreover, notice that:

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]}{\sqrt{1-\omega}}\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:AWGN-model}}}}{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]-\sqrt{\omega}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]}{{1-\omega}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}\mathsf{X}_{\star}]-\sqrt{\omega}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]}{{1-\omega}}=\frac{\sqrt{\omega}(1-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)])}{1-\omega}\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:mmse-pythagoras}}}}{=}\frac{\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}{1-\omega}.

In the above display, step (a) again uses the tower property
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)^{2}]
. Finally, we substitute the formula for
\beta
obtained in the above display in (
77
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}-\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}

after rearrangement. This is exactly the third claim made in the lemma.

Case 2:
\omega=1

When
\omega=1
,
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{X}_{\star}
. The estimator
f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})=\mathsf{X}
has zero MSE and satisfies the divergence-free requirement
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{X}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}]\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}]=0
. Hence,
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(x;a)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}x
is the DMMSE estimator. Moreover, the identity (which is the second claim made in the lemma):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)]=1-\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)

holds trivially in this case since
\mathsf{X}_{\star}=\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A}|\omega)=\mathsf{X}
,
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(1)=0
, and
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}^{2}]=1
.

To finish the proof of the lemma, we need to prove the claim in (
70
).

Proof of (
70
)

We prove the contrapositive of (
70
):
\beta\sqrt{\omega}=1\implies\omega=1
. When
\beta\sqrt{\omega}=1
, (
69
) shows that for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})
which satisfies the divergence-free requirement
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]=0
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})f(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})]=0
. Taking
f=\widehat{\varphi}
(cf. (
68
)), we conclude that
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\varphi}^{2}]=0
. Recalling the definition of
\widehat{\varphi}
, we obtain the following formula for the MMSE estimator for the scalar Gaussian channel:

\displaystyle{\varphi}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})=\beta\mathsf{X}=\frac{\mathsf{X}}{\sqrt{\omega}},

which gives us the following formula for the MMSE:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\omega}{\omega}.

Since
\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})
is bounded by the MSE of the linear estimator
\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathsf{X}
(this is the linear estimator with the lowest MSE),

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X};\mathsf{A})=\frac{1-\omega}{\omega}\leq\mathbb{E}[(\sqrt{\omega}\mathsf{X}-\mathsf{X}_{\star})^{2}]=1-\omega.

Since
\omega\in[0,1]
, by rearranging the above inequality
we conclude that
\omega=1
.
∎

A.2.2
Proof of Lemma
3

Proof of Lemma
3
.

It will be convenient to define the random vector
\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t})^{\top}
and the random variable
\mathsf{S}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left\langle{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle
. Observe that the conditional joint distribution of
(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t},\mathsf{S})
given
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
is:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}\\
\mathsf{S}\end{bmatrix}\;\Big|\;(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mathsf{X}_{\star}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\\
\sqrt{\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma&\Sigma v_{\mathrm{opt}}\\
v_{\mathrm{opt}}^{\top}\Sigma&\,{1-\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\end{bmatrix}\right).

Using Gaussian conditioning, we find that the conditional distribution of
\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}
given
(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
is given by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}\;|\;(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\frac{\mathsf{S\alpha}}{\sqrt{\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}},\Sigma-\frac{1-\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}{\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\cdot\alpha\alpha^{\top}\right).

Since the RHS does not have any dependence on
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
, this implies that
\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}
and
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
are conditionally independent given
\mathsf{S}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}\perp(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})\;|\;\mathsf{S}.

(78)

Analysis of MMSE

We first prove the MMSE formula and identify the MMSE estimator. To do so, we begin by showing the lower bound:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})=\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\geq\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}).

To do so, we consider any
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{\geq}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A}]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:cond-indp}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{A}]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{\geq}\min_{g\in L^{2}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-g(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(c)}}}{=}\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}),

where step (a) used Jensen’s inequality, step (b) follows by observing
\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{A}]\in L^{2}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})
, and step (c) is justified by noticing that
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})
forms a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Since
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})
was arbitrary, we can minimize the LHS of the above display with respect to
f
, which yields:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\geq\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}).

(79)

To obtain the upper bound, we observe that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\varphi(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{\geq}\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A}),

(80)

where the equality in (a) follows by recalling the formula for the MMSE estimator in a scalar Gaussian channel and the inequality in (b) follows by observing that:

\varphi(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})=\varphi(\left\langle{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}f_{\star}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A}).

Combining the conclusions of (
79
) and (
80
) yields:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f_{\star}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}),

which gives us the claimed formula for the MMSE and the MMSE estimator.

Analysis of DMMSE

We begin by observing that since
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{S}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left\langle{v_{\mathrm{opt}}},{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A})
form a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}
, we can write
\mathsf{S}
as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left\langle{v_{\mathrm{opt}}},{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}}\right\rangle=\sqrt{\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sqrt{1-\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{W},\quad\mathsf{W}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\left\langle{v_{\mathrm{opt}}},{\mathsf{Z}_{\leq t}}\right\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right).

(81)

As before, we begin by showing the lower bound:

\displaystyle\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left(\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\;\text{subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})]=0\;\forall\;i\;\in\;[t]\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}).

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})
which satisfies the divergence-free constraints
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})]=0\;\forall\;i\;\in\;[t]
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{\geq}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A}]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:cond-indp}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{A}]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{\geq}\min_{g\in L^{2}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})}\left(\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-g(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\text{ subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{W}g(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})]=0\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{}}}{=}\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}).

In the above display:

1.

Step (a) uses Jensen’s inequality.

2.

In step (b) we observed that
\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{A}]\in L^{2}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})
. Moreover, the estimator
\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{A}]
satisfies the divergence-free constraint
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{W}\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{A}]]=0
. This can be verified as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{W}\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{A}]]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:cond-indp}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{W}\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{X}_{\star}\mathsf{A}]]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(i)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{W}f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})|\mathsf{S},\mathsf{X}_{\star}\mathsf{A}]]=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{W}f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})]\overset{\text{\tiny{(ii)}}}{=}0,

where we used the fact that
\mathsf{W}
is measurable with respect to
\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{S}
(recall (
81
)) in step (i). To obtain the equality marked (ii), we expressed
\mathsf{W}
as a linear combination of
\mathsf{Z}_{\leq t}
(recall (
81
)) and used the fact that
f
satisfies the divergence-free constraints.

Since
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})
was an arbitrary function that satisfies the divergence-free constraints, we can minimize the LHS of the above display with respect to
f
and obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\geq\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}).

(82)

To obtain the upper bound, we observe that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{\geq}\left(\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\text{ subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}f(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})]=0\;\forall\;i\;\in\;[t]\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A}).

(83)

In the above display:

1.

The equality in (a) follows by recalling the formula for the DMMSE estimator in a scalar Gaussian channel (Lemma
2
).

2.

To obtain the inequality in (b), we observed that:

\displaystyle\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})=\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\left\langle{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{\star}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A}).

Moreover,
\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{\star}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\left\langle{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})
satisfies the divergence-free requirements:

\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\left\langle{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})]=0\quad\forall\;i\ \in[t].

This can be verified as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\left\langle{\mathsf{X}_{\leq t}},{v_{\mathrm{opt}}}\right\rangle;\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}})]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:scalarized-channel}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{Z}_{i}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}\left(\sqrt{\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sqrt{1-\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{W};\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}\left(\sqrt{\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sqrt{1-\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{W};\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}|\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A},\mathsf{W}]]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(i)}}}{=}c\mathbb{E}[\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}\left(\sqrt{\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sqrt{1-\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathsf{W};\mathsf{A}|\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}\right)\cdot\mathsf{W}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(ii)}}}{=}0,

where the equality marked (i) follows by observing that
(\mathsf{Z}_{i},\mathsf{W})
are centered Gaussian random variables independent of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})
and hence
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}|\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A},\mathsf{W}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}|\mathsf{W}]=c\mathsf{W}
for some constant
c
(the exact formula for
c
is not important for the argument). The equality (ii) follows because
\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})
satisfies the divergence-free requirement
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{W}\bm{\bar{}}{\varphi}(\mathsf{S};\mathsf{A})]=0
.

Combining the conclusions of (
82
) and (
83
) yields:

\displaystyle\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-\bm{\bar{}}{f}_{\star}(\mathsf{X}_{\leq t};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]=\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega_{\mathrm{eff}}),

which gives us the claimed formula for the DMMSE and the DMMSE estimator. This concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎

A.2.3
Proof of Lemma
4

Proof of Lemma
4
.

It is sufficient to show that for any
\rho\in[0,1]
and any
\omega\in[0,1]
,
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)\leq\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\rho\omega)
. Consider a scalar Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{0};\mathsf{A})
at SNR
\omega
:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})\sim\pi,\quad\mathsf{X}_{0}=\sqrt{\omega}\cdot\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sqrt{1-\omega}\cdot\mathsf{Z}_{0},\quad\mathsf{Z}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right),

where
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}_{0}
are sampled independently. Let
\mathsf{W}
be a
\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)
random variable, sampled independently of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}_{0}
. We introduce the random variable
\mathsf{X}_{1}=\sqrt{\rho}\cdot\mathsf{X}_{0}+\sqrt{1-\rho}\cdot\mathsf{W}
. This construction ensures that
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})
is a scalar Gaussian channel with SNR
\rho\omega
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{X}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\rho\omega}\cdot\mathsf{X}_{\star}+\sqrt{1-\rho\omega}\cdot\mathsf{Z}_{1},\quad\mathsf{Z}_{1}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\sqrt{\rho(1-\omega)}\cdot\mathsf{Z}_{0}+\sqrt{1-\rho}\cdot\mathsf{W}}{\sqrt{1-\rho\omega}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right).

(84)

To obtain the claim of the lemma, we observe that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{0},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left(\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{0},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{0},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\;\text{subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{i}f(\mathsf{X}_{0},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]=0\;\forall\;i\;\in\;\{0,1\}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{\leq}\left(\min_{f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})}\mathbb{E}[\{\mathsf{X}_{\star}-f(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})\}^{2}]\;\text{subject to }\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{1}f(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]=0\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\rho\omega).

In the above display, the equality in step (a) follows from Lemma
3
(the effective SNR of the Gaussian channel
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{0},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})
is
\omega
) and the inequality in step (b) follows by observing that the feasible set in the definition of
\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})
is a subset of the feasible set in the definition of
\mathrm{DMMSE}(\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{X}_{0},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})
. Indeed if
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{1}f(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]=0
, then
f\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X}_{0},\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})
, and:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{0}f(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{0}|\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{Z}_{1},\mathsf{A}]]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{(i)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Z}_{0}|\mathsf{Z}_{1}]]\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:degraded-channel}}}}{=}\frac{\sqrt{1-\rho\omega}}{\sqrt{\rho(1-\omega)}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{X}_{1};\mathsf{A})\mathsf{Z}_{1}]=0,

where the equality marked (i) follows by observing that
(\mathsf{Z}_{0},\mathsf{Z}_{1})
are centered Gaussian random variables independent of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star};\mathsf{A})
(cf. (
84
)). This shows that
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\cdot)
is non-increasing. The continuity of
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\cdot)
on
[0,1)
follows by expressing
\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\cdot)
in terms of
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\cdot)
using Lemma
2
(item (3)) and the continuity of
\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\cdot)
(Fact
2
).
∎

Appendix B
State Evolution for OAMP Algorithms (Theorem
1
)

In this appendix, we present the proof of Theorem
1
. We begin by introducing the key ideas involved in the proof in the form of some intermediate results.

Polynomial Approximation

Consider a general OAMP algorithm:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a})\quad\forall\;t\in\mathbb{N}.

(85)

By a polynomial approximation argument, we may assume that the matrix denoisers
\{\Psi_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are polynomials. This argument is summarized in the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to Appendix
B.1
.

Lemma 5
.

It is sufficient to prove Theorem
1
under the additional assumption that for each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the matrix denoiser
\Psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a polynomial.

Orthogonal Decomposition

Let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{X}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}};\mathsf{A})
denote the state evolution random variables associated with the OAMP algorithm in (
85
). A key idea involved in the proof of Theorem
1
is to consider the following decomposition of the functions
(f_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
:

\displaystyle f_{t}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{t-1};a)\overset{\text{\tiny{}}}{=}\alpha_{t}x_{\star}+f_{t}^{\perp}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{t-1};x_{\star},a),

(86)

where:

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{A})],\quad f_{t}^{\perp}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{t-1};x_{\star},a)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}f_{t}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{t-1};a)-\alpha_{t}x_{\star}.

Notice that by construction,
f_{t}^{\perp}
is orthogonal to the signal state evolution random variable
\mathsf{X}_{\star}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}f_{t}^{\perp}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t-1};\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle=0.

(87)

Using this decomposition, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t}\cdot\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}+\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}^{\perp}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a}).

(88)

Recall that the observed matrix
\bm{Y}
consists of a signal part and a noise part:
\bm{Y}=\frac{\theta}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\top}+\bm{W}
. The decomposition above allows us to disentangle the action of the signal component and the noise component using the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to Appendix
B.2

Lemma 6
.

Let
\Psi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a polynomial function.

1.

There exists a polynomial function
\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
associated with
\Psi
such that
\bm{\Psi}(\bm{Y})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}\;\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\;\widetilde{\Psi}(\bm{W})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}
, where
\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}
denotes asymptotic equivalence of random vectors (Definition
1
).

2.

Let
\bm{v}
be a
N
-dimensional random vector with the property that:

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle{\bm{W}^{i}\bm{v}},{\bm{x}_{\star}}\right\rangle}{N}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}0\quad\forall\;i\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

(89)

Then,
\bm{\Psi}(\bm{Y})\cdot\bm{v}\;\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\;{\Psi}(\bm{W})\cdot\bm{v}
.

We will use the lemma above to show that
f_{t}^{\perp}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})
does not interact with the signal component of
\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})
, thanks to the orthogonality property in (
87
), which will guarantee that
\bm{v}=f_{t}^{\perp}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})
satisfies the orthogonality condition in (
89
). Formally, we will show that:

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}^{\perp}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\Psi_{t}(\bm{W})\cdot f_{t}^{\perp}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a}),

(90a)

On the other hand,
\bm{x}_{\star}
does interact with the signal component in
\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})
and this interaction is captured by the
transformed polynomial

\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}
associated with
\Psi_{t}
, whose existence is guaranteed by the lemma above:

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}.

(90b)

Auxiliary OAMP Algorithm

Using the approximations from (
90
) in the update equation of the OAMP algorithm given in (
88
), leads us to introduce an auxiliary OAMP algorithm which generates iterates
\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{1},\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{2},\dotsc
via the update rule:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t}\cdot\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}+\Psi_{t}(\bm{W})\cdot f_{t}^{\perp}(\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{1},\dotsc,\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

(91)

This auxiliary OAMP algorithm will serve as an easy-to-analyze approximation to the original OAMP algorithm in (
85
). Since the auxiliary OAMP algorithm uses the rotationally invariant noise matrix
\bm{W}
in its iterations (rather than the non-rotationally invariant matrix
\bm{Y}
used by the original OAMP algorithm), its dynamics can be easily analyzed using known results on the state evolution of AMP algorithms for rotationally invariant matrices and the associated universality class
[
50
,
69
,
75
,
29
,
25
,
77
,
26
]
. Applying these results requires us to compute:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\Tr[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N},\quad\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\Tr[\widetilde{\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W})\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}\quad\forall\;s,t\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

At first glance, computing the above limits appears challenging as the transformed polynomials
(\widetilde{\Psi}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
constructed in Lemma
6
have a complicated recursive characterization. Fortunately, the above limits have a simple formula in terms of
\nu
, the limiting spectral measure of
\bm{Y}
in the direction of the signal (recall Lemma
1
).

Lemma 7
.

For any
s,t\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\Tr[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})],

\displaystyle\frac{\Tr[\widetilde{\Psi}_{s}(\bm{W})\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})]}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}_{s}(\mathsf{\Lambda})\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{s}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})\Psi_{t}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})],

where
\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu
and
\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu
.

The proof of this lemma is presented in Appendix
B.3
. An immediate consequence of this lemma and an existing result on the dynamics of AMP algorithms driven by rotationally invariant matrices
[
26
, Theorem 2]
is the following characterization of the dynamics of the auxiliary OAMP algorithm.

Lemma 8
.

1.

For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the iterates generated by the auxiliary OAMP algorithm in (
91
) satisfy

(\bm{x}_{\star},\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{1},\dotsc,\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t};\bm{a})\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A}),

where
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
are the state evolution random variables associated with the original OAMP algorithm in (
85
).

2.

Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
and any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle{\bm{W}^{i}f^{\perp}_{t}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})},{\bm{x}_{\star}}\right\rangle}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}0.

The proof of the above lemma is deferred to Appendix
B.4
. We have now introduced all the key ideas involved in the proof of Theorem
1
and are in a position to present its proof.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Our goal is to show that for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle(\bm{x}_{\star},{\bm{x}}_{1},\dotsc,{\bm{x}}_{t};\bm{a})\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A}).

In light of Lemma
8
, it suffices to show that the auxiliary OAMP algorithm in (
91
) approximates the given OAMP algorithm in the sense:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}\;\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\;\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t}\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

(92)

We show the above claim by induction. As our induction hypothesis, we assume that
\bm{x}_{s}\;\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}\;\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{s}
for all
s<t
, and verify the claim at iteration
t
:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle\;\;\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:original-OAMP-decomp}}}}{=}\;\;\alpha_{t}\cdot\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}+\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}^{\perp}(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}}}\;\alpha_{t}\cdot\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}+\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}^{\perp}(\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{1},\dotsc,\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\overset{\text{\tiny{$N\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\simeq}}}\;\alpha_{t}\cdot\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\bm{W})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}+\Psi_{t}(\bm{W})\cdot f_{t}^{\perp}(\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{1},\dotsc,\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t-1};\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{a})

\displaystyle\;\;\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:aux-OAMP}}}}{=}\;\;\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t}

In the above display, step (a) follows from the induction hypothesis and the Lipschitz continuity of
f_{t}^{\perp}
(which is implied by the Lipschitz continuity of
f_{t}
; see Definition
3
and (
86
)). In step (b), we appealed to Lemma
6
. Indeed, Lemma
8
(claim (2)) guarantees that the orthogonality requirement (
89
) imposed in Lemma
6
is met. This proves the claim (
92
) by induction and concludes the proof of Theorem
1
.
∎

B.1
Proof of Lemma
5

Proof of Lemma
5
.

To prove Lemma
5
, we assume that the claim of Theorem
1
holds under the additional assumption that the matrix denoisers used in the OAMP algorithm are polynomial functions. We will show that Theorem
1
continues to hold even without this additional assumption. To this end, we consider a general OAMP algorithm (recall Definition
3
):

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{t}(\bm{Y})\cdot f_{t}({\bm{x}}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t-1};\bm{a})\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},

(93)

where the matrix denoisers
(\Psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are possibly non-polynomial functions. Let
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\{\mathsf{X}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}};\mathsf{A})
denote the state evolution random variables associated with the OAMP algorithm above. Our goal is to show that:

\displaystyle(\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t};\bm{a})\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

Polynomial Approximation

We begin by approximating the matrix denoisers
\Psi_{t}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
by polynomials. Recall from Assumption
1
, that there is an
N
-independent finite constant
C
such that
\|\bm{W}\|_{\op}\leq C
. Furthermore, thanks to Assumption
1
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{Y}\|_{\op}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\theta}{n}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\top}+\bm{W}\right\|_{\op}\leq C+\frac{\theta\cdot\|\bm{x}_{\star}\|^{2}}{N}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}C+\theta.

Hence, the event:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{N}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\{\|\bm{Y}\|_{\op}\leq K\}\quad\text{ with }\quad K\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}C+\theta+1

occurs with probability tending to
1
. We restrict ourselves to this good event in the remainder of the proof. For each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the Weierstrass Approximation Theorem guarantees the existence of a sequence of approximating polynomial functions
(\Psi_{t}^{(D)})_{D\in\mathbb{N}}
of increasing degree, where
\Psi_{t}^{(D)}
is a degree
D
polynomial, with the approximation guarantee:

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{|\lambda|\leq K}\left|\Psi_{t}^{(D)}(\lambda)-\Psi_{t}(\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle=0,

(94)

Consider the OAMP algorithm which uses the degree-
D
approximations
(\Psi_{t}^{(D)})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
as the matrix denoisers:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}^{(D)}

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{t}^{(D)}(\bm{Y})-\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{t}^{(D)}(\mathsf{\Lambda})]\bm{I}_{N}\right)\cdot\left(f_{t}(\bm{x}_{<t}^{(D)};\bm{a})-\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\mathbb{E}[\partial_{s}f_{t}(\mathsf{X}_{<t}^{(D)};\mathsf{A})]\bm{x}_{s}^{(D)}\right)\ \forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},

(95)

where
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{X}_{t}^{(D)})_{t\in\mathbb{N}};\mathsf{A})
are the state evolution random variables associated with the OAMP algorithm above. Since we assume that Theorem
1
holds for OAMP algorithms with polynomial matrix denoisers, we have that:

\displaystyle(\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{x}_{1}^{(D)},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{t}^{(D)};\bm{a})\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(D)},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t}^{(D)};\mathsf{A})\quad\forall\;t\;\in\;\mathbb{N},\;D\;\in\;\mathbb{N}.

We make the following claims regarding the polynomial approximation to the original OAMP algorithm and its state evolution random variables.

Claim 1

(Convergence of State Evolution Random Variables)
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(D)},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t}^{(D)};\mathsf{A})\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})\quad\text{as $D\rightarrow\infty$},

where
\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}
denotes Wasserstein-
2
convergence for random variables. Formally, this means that for any test function
h:\mathbb{R}^{t+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, independent of
N
, which satisfies the smoothness condition:

\displaystyle|h(x;a)-h(x^{\prime};a^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq L\cdot(\|x-x^{\prime}\|+\|a-a^{\prime}\|)\cdot(1+\|x\|+\|x^{\prime}\|+\|a\|+\|a^{\prime}\|)

(96)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\forall\;x,x^{\prime}\;\in\;\mathbb{R}^{t+1},\;a,a^{\prime}\;\in\;\mathbb{R}^{k},

for some
L<\infty
, we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[h(\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(D)},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t}^{(D)};\mathsf{A})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})].

Claim 2

(Convergence of Iterates)
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{D\rightarrow\infty}\plimsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{(D)}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=0.

We first prove Lemma
5
assuming the above results. To do so, we consider a test function
h:\mathbb{R}^{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, which satisfies the smoothness hypothesis in (
96
) and show that:

\displaystyle\plim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}h(x_{\star}[i],x_{1}[i],\dotsc,x[t];a[i])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}h(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A}).

Notice that for any
D\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the decomposition:
