On the other hand, the Stieltjes transform of
\mu
for the trace ensemble with a polynomial potential
V
satisfies
[
61
, Theorem 11.2.1]

{S}_{\mu}^{2}(z)-V^{\prime}(z){S}_{\mu}(z)+Q(z)=0,\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R},

where the function
Q
is defined in (
230
). By the Sohotsky-Plemelj formula
[
61
, Section 2.1]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{S}_{\mu}(\lambda+\imath\epsilon)=\pi{H}_{\mu}(\lambda)-\imath\cdot\pi\mu(\lambda),\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

Combining the above three equations leads to the following equation:

\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}-V^{\prime}(\lambda)\cdot\frac{1}{\theta}+Q(\lambda)=0,\quad\forall\lambda\in S.

Therefore,

\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}1-\theta\cdot V^{\prime}(\lambda)+\theta^{2}\cdot Q(\lambda)=0,\quad\forall\lambda\in S.

Hence,
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)=\phi(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in S
, which completes the proof of (
233
).
To check that
{\phi}_{\text{poly}}
is a degree
k-1
polynomial, we note that
V^{\prime}(\lambda)
is a degree
k-1
polynomial and
Q(\lambda)
in (
229
) is a degree
k-2
polynomial.

∎

Lemma
17
shows that
\phi(\lambda)
agrees with a polynomial
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\text{Supp}(\mu)
, but they are generally different outside of the support. The following proposition shows that we can replace
\phi(\cdot)
by the polynomial
{\phi}_{\text{poly}}(\cdot)
in the optimal OAMP algorithm with no performance loss asymptotically.

Proposition 6

(A variant of optimal OAMP)
.

Assume that
\bm{W}
is drawn from the trace ensemble with a polynomial potential. Consider a variant of the optimal OAMP algorithm from (
17c
) where
\phi(\cdot)
in the matrix denoiser is replaced by
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\cdot)
. Then, Proposition
1
still holds for this OAMP algorithm.

Proof.

First of all, the variant of the optimal OAMP algorithm satisfies the required trace-free condition:
\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{\text{poly}}(\mathsf{\Lambda};\rho)\right]=0
, where

\Psi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda;\rho)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}1-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi_{\text{poly}}(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi_{\text{poly}}(\mathsf{\Lambda})+\rho}\right]\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)}{\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)+\rho},\quad\forall\;\lambda\;\in\;\mathbb{R},\;\rho\;\in\;(0,\infty).

Therefore, the state evolution framework applies to this variant of optimal OAMP.

To prove that the state evolution characterization claimed in Proposition
1
holds for this new variant of the optimal OAMP algorithm it suffices to verify that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi_{\text{poly}}}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu};\rho)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}_{\star}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})],\quad\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi^{2}_{\text{poly}}}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu};\rho)]=\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}^{2}_{\star}(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu};\rho)]

(236a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi_{\text{poly}}}(\mathsf{\Lambda};\rho)],

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}_{\star}(\mathsf{\Lambda};\rho)],\quad\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi^{2}_{\text{poly}}}(\mathsf{\Lambda};\rho)]=\mathbb{E}\big[{\Psi}^{2}_{\star}(\mathsf{\Lambda};\rho)].

(236b)

This is because, if
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},(\mathsf{X}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}};\mathsf{A})
represent the state evolution random variables associated with the new variant of the optimal OAMP algorithm, then the joint distribution of
(\mathsf{X}_{\star},\mathsf{X}_{t};\mathsf{A})
is determined only by the quantities in (
236
) (cf. Definition
3
). Moreover, the claims in (
236
) are immediate from item 3 of Lemma
17
, which shows that
\phi,\phi_{\text{poly}}
agree on
\text{Supp}(\mu)
and
\text{Supp}(\nu)
, and hence,
\Psi_{\star}=\Psi_{\text{poly}}
on
\text{Supp}(\mu)
and
\text{Supp}(\nu)
.
∎

We next define a function closely related to
{\phi}_{\text{poly}}
, which recovers the matrix processing function used in the Bayes-optimal AMP (BAMP) algorithm proposed in
Barbier et al. [7]
.

Definition 6

(Definition of
J(\lambda)
)
.

Let
k\geq 2
and
V:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a degree
k
polynomial. Let the degree
k-1
polynomial
{\phi}_{\text{poly}}
in (
231
) be represented as

{\phi}_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)=C_{\phi}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}C_{\phi,i}\lambda^{i},\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

(237a)

We define the function
J:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
as

J(\lambda)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}C_{\phi,i}\lambda^{i},\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

(237b)

Examples of
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)
and
J(\lambda)

We now provide explicit formulas for the functions
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)
and
J(\lambda)
for three polynomial potential functions considered in
Barbier et al. [7]
.

1.

Quadratic potential
:

\begin{split}V(\lambda)&=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}.\end{split}

(238a)

This corresponds to the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble (GOE). The density function and its Hilbert transform are
\mu(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{4-\lambda^{2}}
and
{H}_{\mu}(\lambda)=V^{\prime}(\lambda)/2\pi
,
\forall\lambda\in[-2,2]
. From (
231
) and (
237b
), we have

\displaystyle\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=1+\theta^{2}-\theta\cdot\lambda,

(238b)

\displaystyle J(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\theta\cdot\lambda.

(238c)

2.

Quartic potential
:

V(\lambda)=\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}}{2}+\frac{\kappa\lambda^{4}}{4},

(239a)

where
\gamma\in[0,1]
is a parameter and
\kappa
is given by:

\kappa=\kappa(\gamma)=\frac{8-9\gamma+\sqrt{64-144\gamma+108\gamma^{2}-27\gamma^{3}}}{27}\in\left[0,\frac{16}{27}\right].

This choice of
\kappa
ensures that the limiting eigenvalue distribution
\mu
has unit variance. Its density function is given by
\mu(\lambda)=(\gamma+2a^{2}\kappa+\kappa\lambda^{2})\sqrt{4a^{2}-\lambda^{2}}/(2\pi)
,
\forall\lambda\in[-2a,2a]
, where
a^{2}:=\big(\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+12\kappa}-\gamma\big)/(6\kappa)
. The functions
\phi_{\text{poly}}
and
J
read
[
66
]

\displaystyle\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\theta^{2}a^{2}(\gamma+2a^{2}\kappa)^{2}+1-\theta\left(\gamma\lambda-\theta\kappa\lambda^{2}+\kappa\lambda^{3}\right),

(239b)

\displaystyle J(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\theta\left(\gamma\lambda-\theta\kappa\lambda^{2}+\kappa\lambda^{3}\right).

(239c)

3.

Purely sestic potential
:

V(\lambda)=\frac{\xi\lambda^{6}}{6},

(240a)

where
\xi=\frac{27}{80}
. Again, this value of
\xi
ensures that the spectral measure
\mu
has unit variance. Its density function is given by
\mu(\lambda)=(6b^{4}\xi+2b^{2}\xi\lambda^{2}+\xi\lambda^{4})\sqrt{4b^{2}-\lambda^{2}}/(2\pi)
,
\forall\lambda\in[-2b,2b]
, where
b^{2}:=2/3
. The functions
\phi_{\text{poly}}
and
J
are given by
[
7
]

\displaystyle\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{27}{50}\theta^{2}+1-\theta\left(-\theta\xi\lambda^{2}-\theta\xi\lambda^{4}+\xi\lambda^{5}\right),

(240b)

\displaystyle J(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\theta\left(-\theta\xi\lambda^{2}-\theta\xi\lambda^{4}+\xi\lambda^{5}\right).

(240c)

Fig.
4
plots the functions
\phi(\cdot)
and
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\cdot)
corresponding to the quartic potential. In this setup, the measure
\mu
is supported on the interval
[-2a,2a]
with
2a\approx 1.7
, and it can be shown that
\nu
has a point mass (corresponding to the outlying eigenvalue of
\bm{Y}
) at
\lambda_{o}\approx 2.3
. We see from Fig.
4
that
\phi(\cdot)
and
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\cdot)
coincide inside the support of
\mu
but differ outside. Moreover, the curves of
\phi(\cdot)
and
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\cdot)
intersect precisely at the location of the outlying eigenvalue:
\phi(\lambda_{o})=\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda_{o})=0
; see the proof of Proposition
6
.

Figure 4:
Plot of
\phi
and
\phi_{\text{poly}}
for the quartic ensemble with
\gamma=0
.
\theta=1.8
.
\text{Supp}(\mu)=[-2a,2a]
with
2a\approx 1.7
. The singular part of the measure
\nu
contains a single point mass at
\lambda_{o}\approx 2.3
.

E.2
Proof of Proposition
2

We will show that any solution
(\rho,\omega)
to the state evolution (SE) fixed point equation:

\displaystyle\omega=1-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}}\right]\right)^{-1}\cdot{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]},\quad\rho=\frac{1}{\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}-1,\quad\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu.

(241)

is also a solution to the replica fixed point equations:

\displaystyle m=1-\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega),

(242a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{H}]=1-m,

(242b)

\displaystyle\chi=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{QH}],

(242c)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}\equiv\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}=\kappa\theta^{2}\left(\frac{m}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}]+\frac{\chi}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{QH}]\right)+\gamma\theta^{2}m,

(242d)

where
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
are two intermediate variables,
\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu
, and the random variables
\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{\Lambda},m,\chi)
,
\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{\Lambda},\rho)
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\kappa\theta^{2}m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\theta^{2}\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}-\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{1-m}\mathbb{E}\left[m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}+\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}\right]+\frac{m}{1-m},

(242e)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left(\rho+\theta^{2}a^{2}(\gamma+2a^{2}\kappa)^{2}+1-\theta\left(\gamma\mathsf{\Lambda}-\theta\kappa\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\mathsf{\Lambda}^{3}\right)\right)^{-1}.

(242f)

The proof for the other direction, namely, each solution to the replica fixed point equation is also a solution to the SE equations is similar and thus omitted.

We will introduce a series of manipulations of the SE equations (
241
) that eventually lead to the replica equations (
242
), for the
quartic potential
case. Our proof consists of the following steps:

1.

In Lemma
18
, we rewrite (
241
) as (
243
). Note that unlike the original SE equation (
241
) which only involves the measure
\mu
, the new formulation (
243
) involves both
\mu
and
\nu
. The introduction of the new formulation (
243
) is somewhat ad hoc, and is only for the purpose of deriving the replica equations as presented in
[
7
]
.

2.

We recognize that the term in (
243a
) that involves
\nu
is a resolvent operator for
J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})
. We will show that the expectation of this term is the limit of
\frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\rho\bm{I}_{N}+C_{\phi}\bm{I}_{N}-J(\bm{Y})\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}
as
N\to\infty
. In the quartic potential case, the function
J(\cdot)
is a degree three polynomial; see (
239c
). It is possible to invoke the matrix inversion lemma to express the term into a resolvent in terms of
J(\bm{W})
plus a low-rank perturbation term, and subsequently calculate the asymptotics of each term involved. This step is summarized in Lemma
20
.

3.

Using Lemma
20
and some algebraic manipulations, we finally arrive at the replica equations we aim to prove in Proposition
2
. The derivations are postponed to Section
E.2.2
.

E.2.1
Auxiliary results

To compare with the replica calculations in
[
7
]
, we rewrite the fixed point equation (
241
) into an equivalent form in the following lemma.

Lemma 18

(Alternative fixed point equations for SE)
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{X}_{\star}|\mathsf{A}]\in(0,1)
. Let
(\omega,\rho)
be a solution to the state evolution fixed point equations (
241
). Then,
(\omega,\rho)
satisfies the following equations:

\displaystyle\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}=\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)\cdot\left(\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)^{-1}-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]\right)^{-1}\right),

(243a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega),

(243b)

where
\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu
and
\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu
.

Proof.

We prove the two equations seperately.

Proof of (
243a
)

We rewrite the first equation of (
241
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\omega}{1-\omega}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)^{-1}-(1+\rho)

(244a)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)\cdot\left(\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)^{-1}-\rho\cdot\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)^{-1}\right).

(244b)

Comparing (
243a
) and (
244a
), we see that it suffices to prove the following

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]=\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right),\quad\forall\rho>0.

(245)

To prove (
245
), we note that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\lambda)}\nu(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\lambda)}\nu_{\perp}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\lambda)}\nu_{\parallel}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\\
&\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot\nu_{\perp}(\mathbb{R})+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\lambda)}\nu_{\parallel}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\\
&=\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot\left(1-\nu_{\parallel}(\mathbb{R})\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\lambda)}\nu_{\parallel}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\\
&\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot\left(1-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\phi(\lambda)}\mu(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\lambda)}\cdot\frac{1}{\phi(\lambda)}\mu(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\\
&=\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right),\quad\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu,\end{split}

where step (a) and (b) are due to items 2 and 3 of Lemma
1
, respectively.

Proof of (
243b
)

From the second equation of (
241
), we have

\rho=\frac{1}{\mathsf{dmmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}-1\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)}-\frac{1}{1-\omega},

where step (a) follows from Lemma
2
. Hence,

\left(\rho+\frac{1}{1-\omega}\right)^{-1}=\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega).

(246)

Now recall the first equation of (
241
):

\begin{split}\omega&=1-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}}\right]\right)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+{\rho}}\right].\end{split}

By simple algebra, we have

\left(\rho+\frac{1}{1-\omega}\right)^{-1}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})+\rho}\right].

(247)

Combining (
246
) and (
247
) leads to (
243b
).

∎

Note that (
243a
) involves the term
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]
, which depends on the function
\phi(\cdot)
. To facilitate further analysis, we rewrite this term using the function
\phi_{\text{poly}}(\lambda)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}C_{\phi}-J(\lambda)
defined in Appendix
E.1
; see Lemma
17
and Definition
6
. Lemma
19
below summarizes this result. Its proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
6
and omitted.

Lemma 19
.

Let
\mu
be the limiting spectral measure associated with the quartic potential. Let
C_{\phi}
and
J(\cdot)
be defined as in Definition (
6
).
Then, the following holds for any
\rho>0
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right],\quad\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu.

(248)

The following lemma reformulates the term
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]
, which involves the measure
\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu
, into a (complicated) expression that only depends on
\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu
.

Lemma 20
.

Consider the quartic potential for which the function
J(\lambda)
is given in (
239c
). Then, the following identity holds for any
\rho>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]=-\frac{1}{(\kappa\theta^{2})^{2}d_{4}+\gamma\theta^{2}+\frac{3\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}+(\kappa\theta^{2})^{2}(2d_{1}d_{3}-3d_{2}^{2})+(\kappa\theta^{2})^{3}(d_{2}^{3}+d_{0}d_{3}^{2}-2d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})-1}{d_{0}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}^{2}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}d_{2}}},

(249)

where
\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu}\sim\nu
, and
\kappa
and
\gamma
are parameters for the quartic potential function and
\theta
is the parameter for the observation model, and

d_{i}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right],\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}.

(250)

Proof.

Recall that the probability measure
\nu
is the limit of the empirical measure
\nu_{N}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\langle{\bm{u}_{i}(\bm{Y})},{\bm{x}_{\star}}\right\rangle^{2}\cdot\delta_{\lambda_{i}(\bm{Y})}
, where
\{\bm{u}_{i}(\bm{Y})\}_{i\in[N]}
and
\{\lambda_{i}(\bm{Y})\}_{i\in[N]}
are the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of
\bm{Y}
. By the convergence of
\nu_{N}
(Lemma
1
), we have

\displaystyle\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(c_{\ast}\bm{I}_{N}-J(\bm{Y})\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{c_{\ast}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right].

(251)

where
c_{\ast}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\rho+C_{\phi}
. Recall that
J(\cdot)
is a degree three polynomial for quartic potential:

J(\lambda)=\theta\left(\gamma\lambda-\theta\kappa\lambda^{2}+\kappa\lambda^{3}\right).

(252)

Based on the above definition of
J(\cdot)
, we can decompose
J(\bm{Y})
as
J(\bm{W})
plus a low-rank perturbation term:

\displaystyle J(\bm{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J\left(\frac{\theta\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}}{N}+\bm{W}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma\theta\bm{W}-\kappa\theta^{2}\bm{W}^{2}+\kappa\theta\bm{W}^{3}+\frac{\gamma\theta^{2}}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}+\frac{1}{N}\kappa\theta^{3}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star}\cdot\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}}{N}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\kappa\theta^{2}\left(\bm{W}^{2}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}+\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}^{2}+\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J(\bm{W})+\frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\gamma\theta^{2}\bm{I}_{N}+\kappa\theta^{2}\bm{W}\right)+\frac{1}{N}\kappa\theta^{2}\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\kappa\theta^{2}\bm{W}^{2}\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}+\frac{1}{N}\kappa\theta^{3}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star}\cdot\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}}{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J(\bm{W})+\frac{1}{N}\bm{P}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}},

where

\displaystyle\bm{P}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left[\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star},\bm{W}^{2}\bm{x}_{\star}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 3},

(253)

\displaystyle\bm{Q}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left[\left(\left(\gamma\theta^{2}+\kappa\theta^{3}\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star}}{N}\right)\bm{I}_{N}+\kappa\theta^{2}\bm{W}^{2}\right)\bm{x}_{\star},\kappa\theta^{2}\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star},\kappa\theta^{2}\bm{x}_{\star}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 3}.

(254)

Hence,

\displaystyle\left(c_{\ast}\bm{I}-J(\bm{Y})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(c_{\ast}\bm{I}-J(\bm{W})-\frac{1}{N}\bm{P}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\left(\bm{A}-\frac{1}{N}\bm{P}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\bm{A}^{-1}+\frac{1}{N}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}\left(\bm{I}_{3}-\frac{\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}}{N}\right)^{-1}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1},

where the matrix
\bm{A}
in step (a) denotes

\bm{A}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}c_{\ast}\bm{I}-J(\bm{W}),

(255)

and step (b) is from the Morrison–Woodbury formula. Using the above identity, we obtain the following representation of the LHS of (
251
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(c_{\ast}\bm{I}-J(\bm{Y})\right)^{-1}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}}{N}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}\left(\bm{I}_{3}-\frac{\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}}{N}\right)^{-1}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}.

(256)

Therefore, to calculate the limit of
\frac{1}{N}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(c_{\ast}\bm{I}-J(\bm{Y})\right)^{-1}
as
N\to\infty
, it suffices to derive the limit of the terms
\frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}
,
\frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 3}
,
\frac{1}{N}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
and
\frac{1}{N}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 1}
, which essentially involves computing the limit of
\frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{W}^{i}\bm{x}_{\star}
, for
i=1,2
; cf. (
253
) and (
254
). For this purpose, we can apply standard results on concentration of quadratic forms of rotationally invariant matrices (see Fact
1
in Appendix
A.1.2
) to obtain

\begin{split}\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{W}^{i}\bm{x}_{\star}&=\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(c_{\ast}\bm{I}-J(\bm{W})\right)^{-1}\bm{W}^{i}\bm{x}_{\star},\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}}{c_{\ast}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}d_{i},\end{split}

(257)

where
\mathsf{\Lambda}\sim\mu
denotes the limiting eigenvalue distribution of
\bm{W}
, and step (a) is from the definition of
c_{\ast}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\rho+C_{\phi}
. Similarly,

\begin{split}\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{W}\bm{x}_{\star}&=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}]=0.\end{split}

(258)

Using (
257
) and (
258
), and recalling the definitions of the matrices
\bm{P}
and
\bm{Q}
in (
253
) and (
254
), we arrive at

\begin{split}\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}&=[d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}],\\
\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{Q}&=[\gamma\theta^{2}d_{0}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2},\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1},\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}],\\
\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{P}&=\begin{bmatrix}\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}+\gamma\theta^{2}d_{0}&\kappa\theta^{2}d_{3}+\gamma\theta^{2}d_{2}&\kappa\gamma^{2}d_{4}+\gamma\theta^{2}d_{2}\\
\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}&\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}&\kappa\theta^{2}d_{3}\\
\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}&\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}&\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

(259)

Combining (
256
), (
257
) and (
259
), and after lengthy but straightforward calculations, we obtain

\begin{split}&\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\ \frac{1}{N}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}_{\star}\left(c_{\ast}\bm{I}-J(\bm{Y})\right)^{-1}\bm{x}_{\star}\\
&=-\frac{1}{(\kappa\theta^{2})^{2}d_{4}+\gamma\theta^{2}+\frac{3\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}+(\kappa\theta^{2})^{2}(2d_{1}d_{3}-3d_{2}^{2})+(\kappa\theta^{2})^{3}(d_{2}^{3}+d_{0}d_{3}^{2}-2d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})-1}{d_{0}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}^{2}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}d_{2}}}.\end{split}

(260)

Combining (
260
) and (
251
) yields the desired identity in (
249
).
∎

E.2.2
Proof of Proposition
2

In what follows, we prove that any solution
(\rho,\omega)
to the state evolution (SE) fixed point equation (
241
), with
\omega\in(0,1),\rho\in(0,\infty)
, is a solution to the replica fixed point equation of (
242
). This is essentially achieved by combining Lemma
18
and Lemma
20
. More specifically, the replica fixed point equations in (
242
), which we aim to prove, can be obtained by replacing the term
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]
in (
243a
) by the alternative representation of
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]
in (
249
), which is valid because of Lemma
19
, together with additional algebraic manipulations.

Before we present the detailed proof, we first introduce a few definitions. Let

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle\equiv\chi(\rho)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{(1-d_{0})(d_{1}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}d_{3}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}d_{2})}{d_{0}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}d_{2}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}^{2}},

(261)

where

\begin{split}d_{i}&\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{H}\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}]\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},\\
\mathsf{H}&\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{\Lambda},\rho)=\frac{1}{\rho+\phi_{\text{poly}}(\mathsf{\Lambda})}=\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{Eqn:phi_quartic}}}}{=}\frac{1}{\rho+\theta^{2}a^{2}(\gamma+2a^{2}\kappa)^{2}+1-\theta\left(\gamma\mathsf{\Lambda}-\theta\kappa\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\mathsf{\Lambda}^{3}\right)}.\end{split}

The rationale for the above definition of
\chi
will be clear from subsequent derivations. The notations
\chi(\rho)
and
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{\Lambda},\rho)
above highlight the fact that
\chi
and
\mathsf{H}
both depend on the variable
\rho
, which is defined to be a solution to the SE fixed point equation. We further define

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\equiv m(\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}1-\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega),

(262)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{\Lambda},\mathsf{H},\rho,\omega)\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\kappa\theta^{2}m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\theta^{2}\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}-\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{1-m}\mathbb{E}\left[m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}+\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}\right]+\frac{m}{1-m}.

(263)

Again, the notation
m(\omega)
and
\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{\Lambda},\mathsf{H},\rho,\omega)
indicate that
m
depends on
\omega
, and
\mathsf{Q}
depends on the random variables
\mathsf{\Lambda}
and
\mathsf{H}
(which is a function of
\rho
) and the variables
(\rho,\omega)
.

To finish the proof, it remains to verify that the variables
\chi
,
m
,
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{Q}
as defined above satisfy the replica fixed point equations (
242
).

Proof of (
242b
)

By the definition of
\mathsf{H}
:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{H}]&\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{eq:replica_fp_JY3_4}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{Eqn:phi_phi_poly_inside}}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{Eqn:OAMP_FP2_b}}}}{=}\mathsf{mmse}_{\pi}(\omega)\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{\eqref{Eqn:m_mmse_app}}}}{=}1-m.\end{split}

This proves (
242b
).

Proof of (
242c
)

We first verify equation (
242b
):
\chi=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{QH}]
. To this end, we will substitute the definitions of
\mathsf{Q}
and
\chi
into
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{QH}]
and then show that it is equal to
\chi
:

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{QH}]\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{\Lambda}\left(\kappa\theta^{2}m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\theta^{2}\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}-\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{1-m}\mathbb{E}\left[m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}+\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}\right]+\frac{m}{1-m}\right)\mathsf{H}\right]\\
&=\kappa\theta^{2}\left(m\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{3}\mathsf{H}]+\chi\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}]-\frac{1}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}]\left(m\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}]+\chi\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}]\right)\right)+\frac{m}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}]\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{=}\kappa\theta^{2}\left(md_{3}+\chi d_{2}-\frac{1}{1-m}d_{1}(md_{2}+\chi d_{1})\right)+\frac{m}{1-m}d_{1}\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(c)}}}{=}\kappa\theta^{2}\left((1-d_{0})d_{3}+\chi d_{2}-\frac{1}{d_{0}}(1-d_{0})d_{1}d_{2}+\chi\frac{1}{d_{0}}d_{1}^{2}\right)+\frac{1-d_{0}}{d_{0}}d_{1}\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(d)}}}{=}\frac{(1-d_{0})(d_{1}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}d_{3}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}d_{2})}{d_{0}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}d_{2}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}^{2}}\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(e)}}}{=}\chi,\end{split}

(264)

where step (a) is from the definition of
\mathsf{Q}
in (
263
); step (b) is from the definition of the shorthand
d_{i}
:
d_{i}=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{i}\mathsf{H}]
; step (c) is due to the identity
d_{0}=1-m
, which further follows from definition
d_{0}=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{H}]
and the equation
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{H}]=1-m
(see (
242b
)); step (d) follows from the definition of
\chi
in (
261
) and straightforward calculations; step (e) is from the definition of
\chi
in (
261
). This completes the proof of (
242c
).

Proof of (
242d
)

The term
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{QH}]
appears on the RHS of (
242d
). We first rewrite it as an explicit function of
(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})
and
\chi
:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{QH}]&=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\left(\kappa\theta^{2}m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}+\kappa\theta^{2}\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}-\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{1-m}\mathbb{E}\left[m\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}+\chi\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}\right]+\frac{m}{1-m}\right)\mathsf{H}\right]\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}\kappa\theta^{2}md_{4}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{3}\chi-\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{1-m}\left(md_{2}^{2}+d_{1}d_{2}\chi\right)+\frac{m}{1-m}d_{2}\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{=}(1-d_{0})\left(\kappa\theta^{2}d_{4}+\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}d_{3}\chi}{1-d_{0}}-\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{d_{0}}\left(d_{2}^{2}+\frac{\chi}{1-d_{0}}d_{1}d_{2}\right)+\frac{1}{d_{0}}d_{2}\right),\end{split}

(265)

where step (a) is from the definition of
\mathsf{Q}
in (
263
); step (b) uses the identity
d_{0}=1-m
, similar to step (c) of (
264
). Using (
265
), we can continue to rewrite the RHS of (
242d
) as a function of
(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})
and
\chi
:

\begin{split}&\text{RHS of }\eqref{Eqn:SE_FP_quartic_3}\\
&=\kappa\theta^{2}\left(\frac{m}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{H}]+\frac{\chi}{1-m}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}\mathsf{H}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{QH}]\right)+\gamma\theta^{2}m\\
&=\kappa\theta^{2}\left(\frac{1}{d_{0}}(1-d_{0})d_{2}+\frac{1}{d_{0}}d_{1}\chi+\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{\Lambda}^{2}\mathsf{QH}]\right)+(1-d_{0})\gamma\theta^{2}\\
&=(1-d_{0})\left((\kappa\theta^{2})^{2}d_{4}+\gamma\theta^{2}+\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{d_{0}(1-d_{0})}\left(d_{1}+\kappa\theta^{2}(d_{0}d_{3}-d_{1}d_{2})\right)\chi+\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{d_{0}}(2d_{2}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}^{2})\right).\end{split}

(266)

Note that
\chi
is itself a function of
(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})
; see (
261
). Substituting (
261
) into (
266
) eliminates the variable
\chi
. After some algebra we finally obtain

\begin{split}&\text{RHS of }\eqref{Eqn:SE_FP_quartic_3}\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(a)}}}{=}(1-d_{0})\bigg((\kappa\theta^{2})^{2}d_{4}+\gamma\theta^{2}\bigg.\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\bigg.+\frac{3\kappa\theta^{2}d_{2}+(\kappa\theta^{2})^{2}(2d_{1}d_{3}-3d_{2}^{2})+(\kappa\theta^{2})^{3}(d_{2}^{3}+d_{0}d_{3}^{2}-2d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})-1}{d_{0}+\kappa\theta^{2}d_{1}^{2}-\kappa\theta^{2}d_{0}d_{2}}+\frac{1}{d_{0}}\bigg)\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(b)}}}{=}(1-d_{0})\left(-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]\right)^{-1}+\frac{1}{d_{0}}\right)\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(c)}}}{=}\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)\left\{-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+C_{\phi}-J(\mathsf{\Lambda}_{\nu})}\right]\right)^{-1}+\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho+\phi(\mathsf{\Lambda})}\right]\right)^{-1}\right\}\\
&\overset{\text{\tiny{(d)}}}{=}\frac{\omega}{1-\omega},\end{split}

(267)

where step (a) is a consequence of (
261
) and (
266
); step (b) is from Lemma
20
; step (c) is from the definition of
d_{0}
(see (
250
)); and step (d) is due to (
243
) (Lemma
18
). This proves (
242d
).

To conclude, we have verified that the stationary point of the state evolution of OAMP
(\rho,\omega)
satisfies equations (
242a
)-(
242d
). The proof for the other direction, namely, each fixed point of (
242
) such that
\rho>0
and
\omega\in(0,1)
also satisfies the SE equations (
241
) is similar and thus omitted.

Appendix F
Numerical Results

This appendix provides the details for the numerical results presented in Section
5
. It also includes additional numerical results not shown in the main paper.

F.1
Data Processing Details for Fig.
3

Both the 1000G and Hapmap3 datasets undergo the preprocessing steps, producing a
2054\times 2054
symmetric matrix for 1000G and a
1397\times 1397
symmetric matrix for Hapmap3. We denote this dataset by
\bm{Y}_{\text{full}}
(which is a
{2504\times 100000}
matrix for the 1000G dataset and a
1397\times 100000
matrix for the Hapmap3 dataset). The processing steps for both datasets are the same, except for the fact that the dimensions of the two datasets are different.
In what follows, we shall focus on the 1000G dataset only.

In our experiments, we randomly select 3000 SNPs and we denote this subsampled dataset
\bm{Y}_{\text{sampled}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2504\times 3000}
matrix. We generate a symmetric noise matrix by applying the following additional processing steps:

1.

We estimate the “ground truth” signal components
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{2504\times 2504}
by forming the covariance matrice using all SNPs from the original datasets. Specifically, the ground truth signal component is defined by
\bm{X}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\bm{Y}_{\text{full}}\bm{Y}_{\text{full}}^{\mathsf{T}}/100000
.

2.

We generate a noise matrix from the covariance matrix of the sub-sampled data with the ground truth signal removed, namely,
\bm{W}_{1}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\bm{Y}_{\text{sampled}}\bm{Y}_{\text{sampled}}^{\mathsf{T}}/3000-\bm{X}
.

3.

We then remove the top 8 principal components (PCs) of
\bm{W}_{1}
(corresponding to the outlying eigenvalues) and let this matrix be
\bm{W}_{2}
. The final noise matrix is obtained by and further centering and scaling the matrix:
\bm{W}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}\sqrt{2504/\|\bm{W}_{3}\|_{F}^{2}}\cdot\bm{W}_{3}
, where
\bm{W}_{3}\overset{\text{\tiny{def}}}{=}(\bm{W}_{2}-\frac{\text{Tr}(\bm{W}_{2})}{2504}\bm{I})
. The idea is the keep the eigenvalues of
\bm{W}_{2}
unchanged, but standarize the eigenvalues.

The final observation matrix for which the OAMP algorithm is applied to is given by
\bm{Y}=({\theta}/{N})\cdot\bm{x}_{\star}\bm{x}_{\star}^{\mathsf{T}}+\bm{W}
, where
\theta=3.5
and
\bm{W}
is generated as described above, and
\bm{x}_{\star}
is randomly sampled from an i.i.d. prior
0.1\mathcal{N}(0,10)+0.9\delta_{0}
. The histogram of the observation matrix with noise matrix generated from the 1000G dataset is plotted in Fig.
5
.

Figure 5:
Histogram of the eigenvalues of the observation matrix with noise matrix generated from the 1000G dataset.

F.2
Additional Numerical Results

We now provide some additional numerical results not shown in the main paper. Fig.
6
compares the following AMP algorithms:

•

OAMP:
The optimal OAMP algorithm proposed in the current paper.

•

BAMP:
The Bayes-AMP algorithm proposed in
[
7
]
.

•

AMP-AP:
The AMP alternating posteriors algorithm proposed in
[
7
]
.

•

AMP:
The AMP algorithm (for rotationally-invariant model) proposed in
[
29
]
.

We remark that both the AMP-AP and AMP algorithms in the above list use a multivariate MMSE signal denoiser (which depends on all previous iterates), while OAMP and BAMP use the single-step MMSE signal denoiser (which only depends on the current iterate). Fig.
6
also includes the state evolution predictions for the performances of the above AMP algorithms, and the conjectured Bayes-optimal performance calculated via the replica method
[
7
]
.

Fig.
6
shows that the state evolution results for all the AMP algorithms well match the simulations. Moreover, the optimal OAMP algorithm indeed achieves the best performance among the above AMP algorithms. We notice that the performance of the AMP-AP algorithm is very close to that of OAMP, suggesting that AMP-AP might enjoy similar optimality properties as the OAMP algorithm proposed in our paper. However, showing this seems challenging since the state evolution of AMP-AP is significantly more complicated.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 6:
Comparison of the performances of the Bayes-optimal estimator, the optimal OAMP algorithm, the BAMP algorithm and AMP-AP algorithms in
[
7
]
. The detailed experimental setups are as follows.
(Left)
Noise spectrum: quartic potential with
\gamma=0
. Signal prior: two points prior with
\epsilon=\frac{1}{8}
.
(Center)
Noise spectrum: quartic potential with
\gamma=0
. Signal prior: two Points prior with
\epsilon=\frac{1}{3}
.
(Right)
Noise spectrum: purely sestic potential. Signal prior: two Points prior with
\epsilon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}
. The signal dimension is
2\times 10^{6}
. All algorithms use random initialization.

Fig.
7
compares the dynamics of the AMP algorithms shown in Fig.
6
. We use the same noise model as before and sample the signal from an i.i.d. two-point prior with
\epsilon=0.5
and
\theta=1.8
. In the experiments, all the AMP algorithms use random initialization. We see that both OAMP and AMP-AP converge to the conjectured Bayes-optimal performance as predicted by the replica method. In contrast, the AMP algorithm of
Fan [29]
is strictly inferior than the replica prediction, as has already been observed in
[
7
]
. We notice that the performance of BAMP degrades after a few iteration. Notice that the performance of BAMP is well predicted by its asymptotic state evolution. Hence, the observed behavior of BAMP is not caused by finite-
N
effects or numerical stability issues and seems to be an inherent limitation of the algorithm.

Figure 7:
MSE performance of various AMP algorithms as a function of the iteration number. The replica conjecture for the Bayes-optimal performance is included for reference.
